From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia

(Redirected from [Information disorder](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Information_disorder&redirect=no "Information disorder"))

False information presented as news

For other uses, see [Fake news (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_(disambiguation) "Fake news (disambiguation)").

![Image 6](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/en/thumb/f/f2/Edit-clear.svg/40px-Edit-clear.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)This article **may be [too long](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Article_size "Wikipedia:Article size") to read and navigate comfortably**. When this tag was added, its [readable prose size](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:SIZERULE "Wikipedia:SIZERULE") was 19,138 words. Consider [splitting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Splitting "Wikipedia:Splitting") content into sub-articles, [condensing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Summary_style "Wikipedia:Summary style") it, or adding [subheadings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help:Section#Subsections "Help:Section"). Please discuss this issue on the article's [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk:Fake_news "Talk:Fake news")._(January 2024)_
[![Image 7: Three running men carrying papers with the labels "Humbug News", "Fake News", and "Cheap Sensation".](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f7/The_fin_de_si%C3%A8cle_newspaper_proprietor_%28cropped%29.jpg/500px-The_fin_de_si%C3%A8cle_newspaper_proprietor_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_fin_de_si%C3%A8cle_newspaper_proprietor_(cropped).jpg)

Reporters with various forms of "fake news" from an 1894 illustration by [Frederick Burr Opper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Frederick_Burr_Opper "Frederick Burr Opper")

**Fake news** is false or misleading information ([misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misinformation "Misinformation"), [disinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disinformation "Disinformation"), [propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda "Propaganda"), and [hoaxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoaxes "Hoaxes")) claiming the aesthetics and legitimacy of [news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News "News").[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guardian12.17.16-1) Fake news often has the aim of [damaging the reputation of a person or entity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Defamation "Defamation"),[[2]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-USNewsWR-2) or making money through [advertising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Advertising "Advertising") revenue.[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:0-3)[[4]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-4) Although false news has always been spread throughout history, the term _fake news_ was first used in the 1890s when sensational reports in newspapers were common.[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-webster-5)[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politico-6) Nevertheless, the term does not have a fixed definition and has been applied broadly to any type of false information presented as news. It has also been used by high-profile people to apply to any news unfavorable to them. Further, [disinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disinformation "Disinformation") involves spreading false information with harmful intent and is sometimes generated and propagated by hostile foreign actors, particularly [during elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_electoral_intervention "Foreign electoral intervention"). In some definitions, fake news includes [satirical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satire "Satire") articles misinterpreted as genuine, and articles that employ [sensationalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sensationalist "Sensationalist") or [clickbait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clickbait "Clickbait")[headlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Headline "Headline") that are not supported in the text.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guardian12.17.16-1) Because of this diversity of types of false news, researchers are beginning to favour information disorder as a more neutral and informative term.[_[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation\_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_] It can spread through [fake news websites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_websites "Fake news websites").

The prevalence of fake news has increased with the recent rise of [social media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_media "Social media"),[[7]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-7) especially the [Facebook News Feed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook_News_Feed "Facebook News Feed"), and this misinformation is gradually seeping into the mainstream media.[[8]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-8) Several factors have been implicated in the spread of fake news, such as [political polarization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_polarization "Political polarization"), [post-truth politics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-truth_politics "Post-truth politics"), [motivated reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motivated_reasoning "Motivated reasoning"), [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias"), and social media [algorithms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algorithm "Algorithm").[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guardian12.17.16-1)[[9]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-9)[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-wired.com-10)[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guardian11.11.16-11)[[12]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-12)

Fake news can reduce the impact of real news by competing with it. For example, a _[BuzzFeed News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BuzzFeed\_News "BuzzFeed News")_ analysis found that the top fake news stories about the [2016 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_U.S._presidential_election "2016 U.S. presidential election") received more engagement on [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook") than top stories from major media outlets.[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Silverman_11/16/2016-13) It also particularly has the potential to undermine trust in serious media coverage. The term has at times been used to cast doubt upon credible news, and U.S. president [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") has been credited with popularizing the term by using it to describe any negative press coverage of himself. It has been increasingly criticized, due in part to Trump's misuse, with the British government deciding to avoid the term, as it is "poorly defined" and "conflates a variety of false information, from genuine error through to foreign interference".[[14]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-14)

Multiple strategies for fighting fake news are actively researched, for various types of fake news. Politicians in certain [autocratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autocratic "Autocratic") and [democratic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy "Democracy") countries have demanded effective self-regulation and legally enforced regulation in varying forms, of social media and web search engines.

On an individual scale, the ability to actively confront false narratives, as well as taking care when sharing information can reduce the prevalence of falsified information. However, it has been noted that this is vulnerable to the effects of [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias"), [motivated reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motivated_reasoning "Motivated reasoning") and other cognitive biases that can seriously distort reasoning, particularly in dysfunctional and polarised societies. [Inoculation theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inoculation_theory "Inoculation theory") has been proposed as a method to render individuals resistant to undesirable narratives. Because new misinformation emerges frequently, researchers have stated that one solution to address this is to inoculate the population against accepting fake news in general (a process termed _[prebunking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information\_disorder#Prebunking)_), instead of continually debunking the same repeated lies.[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-PsychologyToday-15)

## Definition

Fake news is false or misleading information presented as [news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News "News").[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-wired.com-10)[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-NYT-20170623-16) The term as it developed in 2017 is a [neologism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neologism "Neologism") (a new or re-purposed expression that is entering the language, driven by culture or technology changes).[[17]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-NYTneo-17)_Fake news_ is now used by many people as a catch-all, referring to any lies and misrepresentations, from a news distributor or not; further, a few people use the term to condemn creditable news sources they do not like, without otherwise arguing details.

Fake news stories in the old sense, plus misleading headlines, are presented among other stories by news aggregators or political sites, for financial or political gain. There are also [fake news websites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_websites "Fake news websites") which run only stories that have no basis in fact but are presented as being factually accurate.[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Allcott-2017-18) Some satirical sites openly label themselves as fake news or satire, or they may reveal that they are fake only on closer inspection for clues.

Overlapping terms are _[bullshit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bullshit "Bullshit")_, _hoax news_, _pseudo-news_, _alternative facts_, _false news_ and _junk news_.[[19]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-19)

The [National Endowment for Democracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Endowment_for_Democracy "National Endowment for Democracy") defines fake news as "[M]isleading content found on the internet, especially on social media [...] Much of this content is produced by for-profit websites and Facebook pages gaming the platform for advertising revenue" and distinguishes it from disinformation: "[F]ake news does not meet the definition of disinformation or propaganda. Its motives are usually financial, not political, and it is usually not tied to a larger agenda."[[20]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-ned-20)

While most definitions focus strictly on content accuracy and format, current research indicates that the rhetorical structure of the content might play a significant role in the perception of fake news.[[21]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-21)

Michael Radutzky, a producer of CBS _[60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60\_Minutes "60 Minutes")_, said his show considers fake news to be "stories that are probably false, have enormous traction [popular appeal] in the culture, and are consumed by millions of people." These stories are not only found in politics, but also in areas like vaccination, stock values and nutrition.[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-lazer-22) He did not include news that is "invoked by politicians against the media for stories that they don't like or for comments that they don't like" as fake news. Guy Campanile, also a _60 Minutes_ producer said, "What we are talking about are stories that are fabricated out of thin air. By most measures, deliberately, and by any definition, that's a lie."[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-60Overtime-23)

The intent and purpose of fake news is important. In some cases, fake news may be [news satire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News_satire "News satire"), which uses exaggeration and introduces non-factual elements that are intended to amuse or make a point, rather than to deceive.[[1]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guardian12.17.16-1)[[24]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-24)

In the context of the United States of America and its election processes in the 2010s, fake news generated considerable controversy and argument, with some commentators defining concern over it as [moral panic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_panic "Moral panic") or [mass hysteria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mass_hysteria "Mass hysteria") and others worried about damage done to public trust.[[25]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-25)[[26]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-26)[[27]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-27) It particularly has the potential to undermine trust in serious media coverage generally.[[28]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-28) The term has also been used to cast doubt upon credible mainstream media.[[29]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-29)[[30]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-30)

In January 2017, the United Kingdom [House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom "House of Commons of the United Kingdom") commenced a [parliamentary inquiry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliamentary_inquiry_(parliamentary_procedure) "Parliamentary inquiry (parliamentary procedure)") into the "growing phenomenon of fake news".[[31]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-31)

In 2016, [PolitiFact](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PolitiFact "PolitiFact") selected fake news as their Lie of the Year. No single lie stood out, so the generic term was chosen. Also in 2016, Oxford Dictionaries selected _[post-truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-truth "Post-truth")_ as its word of the year and defined it as the state of affairs when "objective facts are less influential in shaping public opinion than appeals to emotion and personal belief."[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-lie2016-32)

### Roots

Main article: [Media pluralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_pluralism "Media pluralism")

[![Image 8](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d4/The_roots_of_%27fake_news%27.png/250px-The_roots_of_%27fake_news%27.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_roots_of_'fake_news'.png)

The roots of "fake news" from UNESCO's World Trends Report[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-unesco-33)

The term _fake news_ gained importance with the electoral context in Western Europe and North America. It is determined by [fraudulent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fraud "Fraud") content in news format and its velocity.[[33]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-unesco-33) According to Bounegru, Gray, Venturini and Mauri, a lie becomes fake news when it "is picked up by dozens of other [blogs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blog "Blog"), retransmitted by hundreds of websites, cross-posted over thousands of social media accounts and read by hundreds of thousands".[[34]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-34)

The evolving nature of online business models encourages the production of information that is "click-worthy" and independent of its accuracy.[[35]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-35)

The nature of trust depends on the assumptions that non-institutional forms of communication are freer from power and more able to report information that [mainstream media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainstream_media "Mainstream media") are perceived as unable or unwilling to reveal. Declines in confidence in much traditional media[[36]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-36) and expert knowledge[[37]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-37) have created fertile grounds for alternative, and often obscure sources of information to appear as authoritative and credible. This ultimately leaves users confused about basic facts.[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-pew1-38)

### Popularity and viral spread

[![Image 9](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/20241116_%22Fake_news%22_%28topic%29_on_Google_Trends.svg/250px-20241116_%22Fake_news%22_%28topic%29_on_Google_Trends.svg.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:20241116_%22Fake_news%22_(topic)_on_Google_Trends.svg)

[Google Trends](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Trends "Google Trends") topic searches began a substantial increase in late 2016, about the time of the U.S. presidential election.[[39]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-FakeNews_GoogleTrends-39)

Fake news has become popular with various media outlets and platforms. Journalists have identified that platforms like [Google](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google "Google") or [Meta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook") profit from the distribution of fake news.[[40]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-40)[[41]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-41) Part of the reason behind the broad circulation of fake news online is that [fake news websites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_website "Fake news website") can be profitable by monetizing them through [online advertising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Online_advertising "Online advertising").[[3]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:0-3)

Researchers at [Pew Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research_Center "Pew Research Center") discovered that over 60% of Americans access news through social media compared to traditional newspaper and magazines.[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-pew2-42) With the popularity of social media, individuals can easily access fake news and disinformation. The rapid spread of false stories on social media during the 2012 elections in Italy has been documented, as has diffusion of false stories on Facebook during the 2016 US election campaign.[[43]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Zhuravskaya-43)

Fake news has the tendency to become viral among the public. With the presence of social media platforms like [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter"), it becomes easier for false information to diffuse quickly. Research has found that false political information tends to spread three times faster than other false news.[[44]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-vosoughi-44) On Twitter, false tweets have a much higher chance of being retweeted than truthful tweets. More so, it is humans who are responsible for disseminating false news and information as opposed to bots and [click farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Click_farm "Click farm"). The tendency for humans to spread false information has to do with human behavior; according to research, humans are attracted to events and information that are surprising and new, and, as a result, cause high arousal in the brain.[[45]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-45)[[46]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-46) Besides, motivated reasoning was found to play a role in the spread of fake news.[[47]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Tsang-2020-47) This ultimately leads humans to retweet or share false information, which are usually characterized with [clickbait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clickbait "Clickbait") and eye-catching titles. This prevents people from stopping to verify the information. As a result, massive online communities form around a piece of false news without any prior fact-checking or verification of the veracity of the information.

Of particular concern regarding viral spread of fake news is the role of super-spreaders. [Brian Stelter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Stelter "Brian Stelter"), the anchor of _Reliable Sources_ at [CNN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN "CNN"), has documented the systematic long-term two-way feedback that developed between President Donald Trump and [Fox News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News "Fox News") presenters. The resultant conditioning of outrage in their large audience against government and the mainstream media has proved a highly successful money-spinner for the TV network.[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-stelter-48)

### Its damaging effects

In 2017, the inventor of the [World Wide Web](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Wide_Web "World Wide Web"), [Tim Berners-Lee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tim_Berners-Lee "Tim Berners-Lee") claimed that fake news was one of the three most significant new disturbing Internet trends that must first be resolved if the Internet is to be capable of truly "serving humanity". The other two new disturbing trends were the recent surge in the use of the Internet by governments for citizen-surveillance purposes, and for [cyberwarfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyberwarfare "Cyberwarfare") purposes.[[49]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-49)

Author [Terry Pratchett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terry_Pratchett "Terry Pratchett"), previously a journalist and press officer, was among the first to be concerned about the spread of fake news on the Internet. In a 1995 interview with [Bill Gates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bill_Gates "Bill Gates"), founder of [Microsoft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microsoft "Microsoft"), he said, "Let's say I call myself the Institute for Something-or-other and I decide to promote a spurious treatise saying the Jews were entirely responsible for the Second World War, and [the Holocaust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Holocaust "The Holocaust") didn't happen, and it goes out there on the Internet and is available on the same terms as any piece of historical research which has undergone [peer review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer_review "Peer review") and so on. There's a kind of parity of esteem of information on the net. It's all there: there's no way of finding out whether this stuff has any bottom to it or whether someone has just made it up". Gates was optimistic and disagreed, saying that authorities on the Internet would index and check facts and reputations in a much more sophisticated way than in print. But it was Pratchett who more accurately predicted how the Internet would propagate and legitimize fake news.[[50]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-50)

When the Internet first became accessible for public use in the 1990s, its main purpose was for the seeking and accessing of information.[[51]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-51) As fake news was introduced to the Internet, this made it difficult for some people to find truthful information. The impact of fake news has become a worldwide phenomenon.[[52]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-52)

[![Image 10](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/d5/Made_for_Advertising_%28MFA%29_website.jpg/250px-Made_for_Advertising_%28MFA%29_website.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Made_for_Advertising_(MFA)_website.jpg)

Digital advertising technologies monetize fake news websites.[[53]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-53)

Fake news is often spread through the use of [fake news websites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_website "Fake news website"), which, in order to gain credibility, specialize in creating attention-grabbing news, which often [impersonate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Website_spoofing "Website spoofing") well-known news sources.[[54]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-54)[[55]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-snopesfieldguide-55)[[56]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-bengilbert-56) Jestin Coler, who said he does it for "fun",[[23]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-60Overtime-23) has indicated that he earned US$10,000 per month from advertising on his fake news websites.[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-60Minutes-57)

Research has shown that fake news hurts social media and online based outlets far worse than traditional print and TV outlets. After a survey was conducted, it was found that 58% of people had less trust in social media news stories as opposed to 24% of people in mainstream media after learning about fake news.[[58]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Reuters-2017-58) In 2019 [Christine Michel Carter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christine_Michel_Carter "Christine Michel Carter"), a writer who has reported on [Generation Alpha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generation_Alpha "Generation Alpha") for _Forbes_ stated that one-third of the generation can decipher false or misleading information in the media.[[59]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-59)

## Types

Claire Wardle of _[First Draft News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First\_Draft\_News "First Draft News")_, has identified seven types of fake news:[[60]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-60)

[![Image 11](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/4c/Bad_example_of_frame_grab.jpg/250px-Bad_example_of_frame_grab.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Bad_example_of_frame_grab.jpg)

[![Image 12](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Rev._Jim_Jones%2C_1977_%28cropped%29.jpg/250px-Rev._Jim_Jones%2C_1977_%28cropped%29.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Rev._Jim_Jones,_1977_(cropped).jpg)

Manipulated content: an intentionally deceptive photoshopped image of [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton "Hillary Clinton") over a 1977 photo of [Peoples Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peoples_Temple "Peoples Temple") cult leader [Jim Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_Jones "Jim Jones")

1.   satire or parody ("no intention to cause harm but has potential to fool")
2.   false connection ("when headlines, visuals or captions don't support the content")
3.   misleading content ("misleading use of information to [frame an issue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spin_(public_relations) "Spin (public relations)") or an individual")
4.   false context ("when genuine content is shared with false contextual information")
5.   impostor content ("when genuine sources are impersonated" with false, made-up sources)
6.   manipulated content ("when genuine information or imagery is manipulated to deceive", as with a "doctored" photo)
7.   fabricated content ("new content is 100% false, designed to deceive and do harm")

[Scientific denialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific_denialism "Scientific denialism") is another potential explanatory type of fake news, defined as the act of producing false or misleading facts to unconsciously support strong pre-existing beliefs.[[61]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-61)

## Criticism of the term

In 2017, Wardle announced she has now rejected the phrase _fake news_ and "censors it in conversation", finding it "woefully inadequate" to describe the issues. She now speaks of _information disorder_ and _information pollution_, and distinguishes between three overarching types of information content problems:[[62]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-62)

1.   [Mis-information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misinformation "Misinformation") (misinformation): false information disseminated without harmful intent.
2.   [Dis-information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disinformation_attack "Disinformation attack") (disinformation): false information created and shared by people with harmful intent.
3.   [Mal-information](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malinformation "Malinformation") (malinformation): the sharing of "genuine" information with the intent to cause harm.

[Disinformation attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disinformation_attack "Disinformation attack") are the most insidious type because of the harmful intent. For example, it is sometimes generated and propagated by hostile foreign actors, particularly [during elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_electoral_intervention "Foreign electoral intervention").[[63]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-63)[[64]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-64)

Because of the manner in which former president Donald Trump has co-opted the term, _[The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Washington\_Post "The Washington Post")_ media columnist [Margaret Sullivan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Margaret_Sullivan_(journalist) "Margaret Sullivan (journalist)") has warned fellow journalists that "It's time to retire the tainted term 'fake news'. Though the term hasn't been around long, its meaning already is lost."[[65]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Gendreau_2/25/2017-65) By late 2018, the term "fake news" had become _verboten_ and U.S. journalists, including the [Poynter Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poynter_Institute "Poynter Institute") were asking for apologies and for product retirements from companies using the term.[[66]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-66)[[67]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-67)[[68]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-68)

In October 2018, the British government decided that the term _fake news_ will no longer be used in official documents because it is "a poorly defined and misleading term that conflates a variety of false information, from genuine error through to foreign interference in democratic processes."[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-telegraphoct18-69) This followed a recommendation by the [House of Commons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Commons_of_the_United_Kingdom "House of Commons of the United Kingdom")' [Digital, Culture, Media and Sport Committee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital,_Culture,_Media_and_Sport_Committee "Digital, Culture, Media and Sport Committee") to avoid the term.[[69]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-telegraphoct18-69)

Researchers have also stated that the narrative of 'fake news' frames the media as a part of the problem rather than the solution. The stereotypes created by those who have manufactured misleading information threaten the integrity of journalists who work to uphold the utmost standards of accuracy.[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:2-70) Many encourage discarding the term altogether, as the British government did. Clearer terms such as 'misinformation' are more useful in helping the public navigate the vast realm of information available thanks to search engines and social media.[[70]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:2-70)

However, recent reviews of fake news still regard it as a useful broad construct, equivalent in meaning to _fabricated news_, as separate from related types of problematic news content, such as [hyperpartisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hyperpartisan "Hyperpartisan") news, the latter being a particular source of political polarization.[[22]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-lazer-22)[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-pennycook-71) Therefore, researchers are beginning to favour _information disorder_ as a more neutral and informative term. For example, the Commission of Inquiry by the [Aspen Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aspen_Institute "Aspen Institute") (2021) has adopted the term _Information Disorder_ in its investigative report.[[72]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-72)

## Identification

[![Image 13](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/2b/How_to_Spot_Fake_News.pdf/page1-250px-How_to_Spot_Fake_News.pdf.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:How_to_Spot_Fake_News.pdf)

Infographic _How to spot fake news_ published by the [International Federation of Library Associations and Institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_of_Library_Associations_and_Institutions "International Federation of Library Associations and Institutions")

According to an academic library guide, a number of specific aspects of fake news may help to identify it and thus avoid being unduly influenced.[[73]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-73) These include: [clickbait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Clickbait "Clickbait"), [propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda "Propaganda"), [satire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satire "Satire")/[parody](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parody "Parody"), sloppy [journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journalism "Journalism"), [misleading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misleading "Misleading") headings, [manipulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manipulation_(psychology) "Manipulation (psychology)"), [rumor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumor "Rumor") mill, [misinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Misinformation "Misinformation"), [media bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_bias "Media bias"), audience bias, and [content farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Content_farm "Content farm").

The [International Federation of Library Associations and Institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Federation_of_Library_Associations_and_Institutions "International Federation of Library Associations and Institutions") (IFLA) published a summary in diagram form _(pictured at right)_ to assist people in recognizing fake news.[[74]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-74) Its main points are:

1.   Consider the source (to understand its mission and purpose)
2.   Read beyond the headline (to understand the whole story)
3.   Check the authors (to see if they are real and credible)
4.   Assess the supporting sources (to ensure they support the claims)
5.   Check the date of publication (to see if the story is relevant and up to date)
6.   Ask if it is a joke (to determine if it is meant to be [satire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satire "Satire"))
7.   Review your own biases (to see if they are affecting your judgment)
8.   Ask experts (to get confirmation from independent people with knowledge).[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-spotfake-75)

The International Fact-Checking Network (IFCN), launched by the Poynter Institute in 2015, supports international collaborative efforts in fact-checking, provides training, and has published a code of principles.[[76]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-76) In 2017 it introduced an application and vetting process for journalistic organisations.[[77]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-77) One of IFCN's verified signatories, the independent, not-for-profit media journal [_The Conversation_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Conversation_(website) "The Conversation (website)"), created a short animation explaining its fact checking process, which involves "extra checks and balances, including [blind peer review](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peer_review "Peer review") by a second academic expert, additional scrutiny and editorial oversight".[[78]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-78)

Beginning in the 2017 school year, children in Taiwan study a new curriculum designed to teach critical reading of propaganda and the evaluation of sources. Called "media literacy", the course provides training in journalism in the new information society.[[79]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Time-Smith-79)

### Online identification

Fake news has become increasingly prevalent over the last few years, with over 100 misleading articles and rumors spread regarding the [2016 United States presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election "2016 United States presidential election") alone.[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Allcott-2017-18) These fake news articles tend to come from satirical news websites or individual websites with an incentive to propagate false information, either as clickbait or to serve a purpose.[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Allcott-2017-18) Since they typically hope to intentionally promote incorrect information, such articles are quite difficult to detect.[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Shu-2017-80)

When identifying a source of information, one must look at many attributes, including but not limited to the content of the email and social media engagements. Specifically, the language is typically more inflammatory in fake news than real articles, in part because the purpose is to confuse and generate clicks.[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Shu-2017-80)

Furthermore, modeling techniques such as [n-gram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N-gram "N-gram") encodings and [bag of words](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bag-of-words_model "Bag-of-words model") have served as other linguistic techniques to determine the legitimacy of a news source.[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Shu-2017-80) On top of that, researchers have determined that visual-based cues also play a factor in categorizing an article, specifically some features can be designed to assess if a picture was legitimate and provides more clarity on the news.[[80]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Shu-2017-80) There are also many social context features that can play a role, as well as the model for spreading the news. Websites such as [Snopes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snopes "Snopes") try to detect this information manually, while certain universities are trying to build mathematical models to do this themselves.[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Allcott-2017-18)

## Tackling and suppression strategies

Considerable research is underway regarding strategies for confronting and suppressing fake news of all types, in particular [disinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disinformation "Disinformation"), which is the deliberate spreading of false narratives for political purposes, or for destabilising [social cohesion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_cohesion "Social cohesion") in targeted communities. Multiple strategies need to be tailored to individual types of fake news, depending for example on whether the fake news is deliberately produced, or rather unintentionally or unconsciously produced.

Considerable [resources](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#Resources:_External_links) are available to combat fake news. Regular summaries of current events and research are available on the websites and email newsletters of a number of support organisations. Particularly notable are the First Draft Archive,[[81]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-81) the Information Futures Lab, School of Public Health, Brown University[[82]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-82) and the Nieman Foundation for Journalism (Harvard University).[[83]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-83)

Journalist Bernard Keane, in his book on misinformation in Australia, classifies strategies for dealing with fake news into three categories: (1) the liar (the perpetrator of fake news), (2) the conduit (the method of carriage of the fake news), and (3) the lied-to (the recipient of the fake news).[[84]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-84)

### Strategies regarding the perpetrator

#### Promotion of facts over emotions

American philosopher of science Lee McIntyre, who has researched the scientific attitude and [post-truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Post-truth "Post-truth"), has explained the importance of factual basis of society, in preference to one in which emotions replace facts. One modern example is the symbiotic relationship that developed between President Donald Trump and [Fox News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News "Fox News"), in which the conspiracy beliefs of Fox hosts were repeated shortly after by Trump (and vice versa) in a continuous feedback loop. This served to promote outrage, and thus to condition and radicalise conservative Republican Fox listeners into cult-like Trump supporters, and to demonise and gaslight Democratic opponents, the mainstream media, and elites generally.[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-stelter-48)

A key strategy to counter fake news based on emotions rather than facts is to flood the information space, particularly social media and web browser search results with factual news, thus drowning out misinformation.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-mcintyre-85) A key factor in establishing facts is the role of critical thinking, the principles of which should be imbedded more comprehensively within all school and university education courses.[[86]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-harrison-86)[Critical thinking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_thinking "Critical thinking") is a style of thinking in which citizens, prior to subsequent [problem solving](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Problem_solving "Problem solving") and [decision-making](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decision-making "Decision-making"), have learned to pay attention to the content of written words, and to judge their accuracy and fairness, among other worthy attributes.

#### Technique rebuttal

Because content rebuttal (presenting true facts to refute false information) does not always work, Lee McIntyre suggests the better method of technique rebuttal, in which faulty reasoning by deniers is exposed, such as cherry-picking data, and relying too much on fake experts. Deniers have a lot of information, but a deficit of trust in mainstream sources. McIntyre first builds trust by respectful exchange, listening carefully to their explanation without interrupting. Then he asks questions such as "What evidence would make you change your mind?" and "Why do you trust that source?" McIntyre has used his technique to talk to [flat earthers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flat_earthers "Flat earthers"), though he admits it may not work with hard-core deniers.[[87]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-87)

#### Individual counteraction

Individuals should confront misinformation when spotted in online blogs, even if briefly, otherwise they fester and proliferate. The person being responded to is probably resistant to change, but many other bloggers may read and learn from an evidence-based reply.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-mcintyre-85) A brutal example was learned by John Kerry during the US 2004 Presidential election campaign against George W. Bush. The right-wing [Swift Boat Veterans for Truth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swift_Vets_and_POWs_for_Truth "Swift Vets and POWs for Truth") falsely claimed that Kerry showed cowardice during the Vietnam War. Kerry refused to dignify the claims with a response for two weeks, despite being pummeled in the media, and this action contributed to his marginal loss to Bush. We should never assume any claim is too outrageous to be believed.[[85]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-mcintyre-85):154–155

However, caution applies regarding over-zealous debunking of fake news. It is often unwise to draw attention to fake news published on a low-impact website or blog (one that has few followers). If this fake news is debunked by a journalist in a high-profile place such as _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_New\_York\_Times "The New York Times")_, knowledge of the false claim [spreads widely](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Streisand_effect "Streisand effect"), and more people overall will end up believing it, ignoring or denying the debunk.[[a]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-backfire-88)

#### Backfire effect

A widely reported paper by Brendan Nyhan and Jason Riefler in 2010 found that when persons with a firm belief are presented with corrective information, their mistaken political beliefs were reinforced rather than reduced in two of their five studies. The researchers called this a [backfire effect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Backfire_effect "Backfire effect").[[88]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-89) However, this finding was widely misreported as that corrective information was the sole cause of reinforced misinformation. Later studies, including by Nyhan and colleagues, failed to support a backfire effect. Instead, Nyhan now accepts that the reinforced beliefs are largely controlled by cues from high-profile elites and media that spread misinformation.[[89]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-90)

### Strategies regarding carriers

#### Regulation of social media

Internet companies with threatened credibility have developed new responses to limit fake news and reduce financial incentives for its proliferation.[[90]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-91)[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-wingfield-92)

A valid criticism of social media companies is that users are [presented with content that they will like](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filter_bubble "Filter bubble"), based on previous viewing preferences. An undesirable side-effect is that [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias") is enhanced in users, which in turn enhances the acceptance of fake news. To reduce this bias, effective self-regulation and legally enforced regulation of social media (notably Facebook and Twitter) and web search engines (notably Google) need to become more effective and innovative.[[91]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-wingfield-92)

Financial disincentives to tackle fake news also apply to some mainstream media. Brian Stelter, the anchor of _Reliable Sources_ at CNN, has provided a substantial critique of the symbiotic but damaging relationship that developed between President Donald Trump and Fox News, which has proved an extraordinarily successful money-spinner for the Murdoch-owned TV network, despite it being a super-spreader of fake news.[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-stelter-48)

#### General strategy

The general approach by these tech companies is the detection of problematic news via human fact-checking and automated artificial intelligence (machine learning, natural language processing and network analysis). Tech companies have utilized two basic counter-strategies: down-ranking fake news and warning messages.[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-pennycook-71)

In the first approach, problematic content is down-ranked by the search algorithm, for example, to the second or later pages on a Google search, so that users are less likely to see it (most users just scan the first page of search results). However, two problems arise. One is that truth is not black-and-white, and fact-checkers often disagree on how to classify the content included in computer training sets, running the risk of false positives and unjustified censorship. Also, fake news often evolves rapidly, and therefore identifiers of misinformation may be ineffective in the future.[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-pennycook-71)

The second approach involves attaching warnings to content that professional fact-checkers have found to be false. Much evidence indicates that corrections and warnings do produce reduced misperceptions and sharing. Despite some early evidence that fact-checking could backfire, recent research has shown that these backfire effects are extremely uncommon. But an important problem is that professional fact-checking is not scalable—it can take substantial time and effort to investigate each particular claim. Thus, many (if not most) false claims never get fact-checked. Also, the process is slow, and a warning may miss the period of peak viral spreading. Further, warnings are typically only attached to blatantly false news, rather than to biased coverage of events that actually occurred.[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-pennycook-71)

A third approach is to place more emphasis on reliable sources such as [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia "Wikipedia"), as well as mainstream media (for example, _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_New\_York\_Times "The New York Times")_ and _[The Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Wall\_Street\_Journal "The Wall Street Journal")_), and science communication publications (for example, _[Scientific American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scientific\_American "Scientific American")_ and [_The Conversation_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Conversation_(website) "The Conversation (website)")). However, this approach has led to mixed results, as hyperpartisan commentary and [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias") is found even in these sources (the media has both news and opinion pages). In addition, some sections of the community completely reject scientific commentary.[[71]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-pennycook-71)

A fourth approach is to ban or specifically target so-called super-spreaders of fake news from social media.[[48]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-stelter-48)

#### Fact-checking

During the 2016 United States presidential election, the creation and [coverage of fake news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_websites_in_the_United_States "Fake news websites in the United States") increased substantially.[[32]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-lie2016-32) This resulted in a widespread [response](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_website#Response "Fake news website") to combat the spread of fake news.[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Snopes-93)[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Forbes-94)[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-NYTimes-95) The volume and reluctance of fake news websites to respond to fact-checking organizations has posed a problem to inhibiting the spread of fake news through fact checking alone.[[95]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-96) In an effort to reduce the effects of fake news, fact-checking websites, including [Snopes.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snopes.com "Snopes.com") and [FactCheck.org](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/FactCheck.org "FactCheck.org"), have posted guides to spotting and avoiding fake news websites.[[92]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Snopes-93)[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-spotfake-75) Social media sites and search engines, such as [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook") and [Google](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google "Google"), received criticism for facilitating the spread of fake news. Both of these corporations have taken measures to explicitly prevent the spread of fake news; critics, however, believe more action is needed.[[94]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-NYTimes-95)

#### Facebook

After the 2016 American election and the run-up to the German election, Facebook began labeling and warning of inaccurate news[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Stelter-2017-97)[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Owen-2016-98) and partnered with independent [fact-checkers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fact_checking "Fact checking") to label inaccurate news, warning readers before sharing it.[[96]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Stelter-2017-97)[[97]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Owen-2016-98) After a story is flagged as disputed, it will be reviewed by the third-party fact-checkers. Then, if it has been proven to be a fake news story, the post cannot be turned into an ad or promoted.[[98]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-99) Artificial intelligence is one of the more recent technologies being developed in the United States and Europe to recognize and eliminate fake news through algorithms.[[93]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Forbes-94) In 2017, Facebook targeted 30,000 accounts related to the spread of misinformation regarding the [French presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_French_presidential_election "2017 French presidential election").[[99]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-100)

In 2020, during the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic"), Facebook found that [troll farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Troll_farm "Troll farm") from [North Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Macedonia "North Macedonia") and the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines") pushed coronavirus disinformation. The publishers that used contents from these farms were banned from the platform.[[100]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-101)

#### Google

In March 2018, [Google](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google "Google") launched [Google News Initiative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_News_Initiative "Google News Initiative") (GNI) to fight the spread of fake news. It launched GNI under the belief that quality [journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journalism "Journalism") and identifying truth online is crucial. GNI has three goals: "to elevate and strengthen quality journalism, evolve business models to drive sustainable growth and empower news organizations through technological innovation".[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Engadget-2018-102) To achieve the first goal, Google created the Disinfo Lab, which combats the spread of fake news during crucial times such as elections or breaking news. The company is also working to adjust its systems to display more trustworthy content during times of breaking news. To make it easier for users to subscribe to media publishers, Google created Subscribe with Google. Additionally, they have created a dashboard, News Consumer Insights that allows news organizations to better understand their audiences using data and analytics. Google was to spend $300 million through 2021 on these efforts, among others, to combat fake news.[[101]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Engadget-2018-102)

In November 2020, [YouTube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouTube "YouTube") (owned by Google) suspended news outlet [One America News Network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/One_America_News_Network "One America News Network") (OANN) for a week for spreading misinformation on coronavirus. The outlet has violated YouTube's policy multiple times. A video that falsely promoted a guaranteed cure to the virus has been deleted from the channel.[[102]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-103)

#### Legal and criminal sanctions in general

The use of anonymously hosted [fake news websites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_website "Fake news website") has made it difficult to prosecute sources of fake news for [libel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libel "Libel").[[10]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-wired.com-10)[[103]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-104)

Numerous countries have created laws in an attempt to regulate or prosecute harmful misinformation more generally than just with a focus on tech companies. In numerous countries, people have been arrested for allegedly spreading [fake news about the COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_misinformation "COVID-19 misinformation").[[b]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-bans-132)

[Algerian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algeria "Algeria") lawmakers passed a law criminalising "fake news" deemed harmful to "public order and state security".[[131]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-133) The [Turkish Interior Ministry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_the_Interior_(Turkey) "Ministry of the Interior (Turkey)") has been [arresting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_Turkey "Censorship in Turkey") social media users whose posts were "targeting officials and spreading panic and fear by suggesting the virus had spread widely in Turkey and that officials had taken insufficient measures".[[132]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-134)[Iran's military](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_the_Islamic_Republic_of_Iran "Armed Forces of the Islamic Republic of Iran") said 3600 people have been arrested for "spreading rumors" about COVID-19 in the country.[[133]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-135) In [Cambodia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambodia "Cambodia"), some individuals who expressed concerns about the spread of COVID-19 have been arrested on fake news charges.[[134]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-136)[[135]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-137) The [United Arab Emirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Emirates "United Arab Emirates") have introduced criminal penalties for the spread of misinformation and rumours related to the outbreak.[[136]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-138)

### Strategies regarding the recipient

#### Cognitive biases of recipient

The vast proliferation of online information, such as in blogs and tweets, has inundated the online marketplace. Because of the resulting [information overload](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_overload "Information overload"), humans cannot process all these information units (called [memes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meme "Meme")), so [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias") and other cognitive biases decide which ones to pay attention to, thus enhancing the spread of fake news. Moreover, these cognitive vulnerabilities are easily exploited by both computer algorithms that present information one may like (based on previous social media use) and by individual manipulators who create social media bots to deliberately spread disinformation.[[137]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-139)

A recent study by Randy Stein and colleagues shows that conservatives value personal stories (non-scientific, intuitive or experiential evidence) more than do liberals (progressives), and therefore perhaps may be less swayed by scientific evidence. This study, however, only tested responses to apolitical messages.[[138]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-stein-140)[[139]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-stein1-141)

#### Nudges as reflection prompts

People tend to react hastily and share fake news without thinking carefully about what they have read or heard, and without checking or verifying the information. "Nudging" people to consider the accuracy of incoming information has been shown to prompt people to think about it, to improve the accuracy of their judgement, and to reduce the likelihood that incorrect information is unreflectively shared.[[140]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Larson-142)[[141]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Pennycook-143)[[142]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Bago-144)[[143]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-145) An example of a technology-based nudge is Twitter's "read before you retweet" prompt, which prompts readers to read an article and consider its contents before retweeting it.[[144]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Vincent-146)

#### Media literacy

Critical [media literacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_literacy "Media literacy") skills, for both printed and digital media, help readers self-evaluate the accuracy of the media content. Nolan Higdon argues that a critical media literacy education focused on teaching critical thinking about how to detect fake news is the most effective method to mitigate the influence of propaganda.[[145]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Higdon-147)

#### Mental immune health, inoculation and prebunking

American philosopher Andy Norman, in his book _Mental Immunity_, argues for a new science of cognitive immunology as a practical guide to resisting bad ideas (such as [conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conspiracy_theories "Conspiracy theories")), as well as transcending petty [tribalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribalism "Tribalism"). He argues that reasoned argument, the scientific method, fact-checking and critical thinking skills alone are insufficient to counter the broad scope of false information. Overlooked is the power of [confirmation bias](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confirmation_bias "Confirmation bias"), [motivated reasoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Motivated_reasoning "Motivated reasoning"), and other cognitive biases that can seriously distort the many facets of mental 'immunity' (public resilience to fake news), particularly in dysfunctional societies.[[146]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-norman-148)

One problem identified by Susan A. Nolan, Ph.D., and Michael Kimball, writing for _[Psychology Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychology\_Today "Psychology Today")_, is that new misinformation, including intentional disinformation, is constantly emerging. The pair cited research suggesting that this could be addressed by inoculating the population against misinformation, rather than to continually having to debunk each new claim at a later time, explaining that this inoculation builds public resilience and creates the conditions for psychological '[herd immunity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Herd_immunity "Herd immunity")'. The general term for this process is **prebunking**, defined as the process of debunking lies, tactics or sources before they strike. The research they cited included studies of free online games shown to provide tools to fight fake news, leading to healthy skepticism when consuming news.[[15]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-PsychologyToday-15)As of 2023[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Fake_news&action=edit), Google implemented novel prebunking video adverts, which have been shown to be effective in countering misinformation during trials in Eastern Europe.[[147]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Fortune-149)

Most current research is based on [inoculation theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inoculation_theory "Inoculation theory"), a social psychological and communication theory that explains how an attitude or belief can be protected against persuasion or influence in much the same way a body can be protected against disease—for example, through pre-exposure to weakened versions of a stronger, future threat. The theory uses inoculation as its explanatory analogy—applied to attitudes (or beliefs) much like a vaccine is applied to an infectious disease. It has great potential for building public resilience ('immunity') against misinformation and fake news, for example, in tackling [science denialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denialism "Denialism"), risky health behaviours, and emotionally manipulative marketing and political messaging.[[148]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-vanderlinden-150)[[149]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-compton2013-151)[[150]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-152)[[151]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-153)[[152]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Compton_2025-154)

For example, John Cook and colleagues have shown that inoculation theory shows promise in countering [climate change denialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change_denialism "Climate change denialism"). This involves a two-step process. Firstly, the 50 or so most common myths about climate change are listed and deconstructed by identifying the reasoning errors and [logical fallacies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Logical_fallacies "Logical fallacies") of each one. Secondly, the concept of parallel argumentation is used to explain the flaw in the argument by transplanting the same logic into a parallel situation, often an extreme or absurd one. Adding appropriate humour can be particularly effective.[[153]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-cook1-155)[[154]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-156)[[155]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-cook2-157)

## History

### Ancient

[![Image 14: stone sculpture of a man's head and neck](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/21/Marcus_Antonius_marble_bust_in_the_Vatican_Museums.jpg/250px-Marcus_Antonius_marble_bust_in_the_Vatican_Museums.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Marcus_Antonius_marble_bust_in_the_Vatican_Museums.jpg)

Roman politician and general [Mark Antony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Antony "Mark Antony") killed himself because of misinformation.[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-MarcAntonysuicide-158)

In the 13th century BC, [Ramesses the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ramesses_II "Ramesses II") spread lies and propaganda portraying the [Battle of Kadesh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Kadesh "Battle of Kadesh") as a stunning victory for the Egyptians; he depicted scenes of himself smiting his foes during the battle on the walls of nearly all his temples. The [treaty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egyptian%E2%80%93Hittite_peace_treaty "Egyptian–Hittite peace treaty") between the Egyptians and the [Hittites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hittites "Hittites"), however, reveals that the battle was actually a [stalemate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stalemate "Stalemate").[[157]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-159)

During the first century BC, [Octavian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Augustus "Augustus") ran a campaign of misinformation against his rival [Mark Antony](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Antony "Mark Antony"), portraying him as a drunkard, a womanizer, and a mere puppet of the Egyptian queen [Cleopatra VII](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cleopatra_VII "Cleopatra VII").[[158]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-160) He published a document purporting to be Mark Antony's will, which claimed that Mark Antony, upon his death, wished to be entombed in the mausoleum of the Ptolemaic pharaohs. Although the document may have been forged, it invoked outrage from the Roman populace.[[159]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-161) Mark Antony ultimately killed himself after his defeat in the [Battle of Actium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Actium "Battle of Actium") upon hearing false rumors propagated by Cleopatra herself claiming that she had committed suicide.[[156]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-MarcAntonysuicide-158)

During the second and third centuries AD, false rumors were spread about Christians claiming that they engaged in ritual [cannibalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_cannibalism "Human cannibalism") and [incest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Incest "Incest").[[160]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-162)[[161]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-163) In the late third century AD, the [Christian apologist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_apologetics "Christian apologetics")[Lactantius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lactantius "Lactantius") invented and exaggerated stories about pagans engaging in acts of immorality and cruelty,[[162]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-164) while the [anti-Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criticism_of_Christianity "Criticism of Christianity") writer [Porphyry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porphyry_(philosopher) "Porphyry (philosopher)") invented similar stories about Christians.[[163]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-165)

### Medieval

In 1475, a fake news story in [Trent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trento "Trento") claimed that the Jewish community had murdered a two-and-a-half-year-old Christian infant named [Simonino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simon_of_Trent "Simon of Trent").[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politico-6) The story resulted in all the Jews in the city being arrested and tortured; 15 of them were burned at the stake.[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politico-6)[Pope Sixtus IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pope_Sixtus_IV "Pope Sixtus IV") himself attempted to stamp out the story; however, by that point, it had already spread beyond anyone's control.[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politico-6) Stories of this kind were known as "[blood libel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blood_libel "Blood libel")"; they claimed that Jews purposely killed Christians, especially Christian children, and used their blood for religious or ritual purposes.[[164]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-166)

### Early modern

After the invention of the [printing press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Printing_press "Printing press") in 1439, publications became widespread but there was no standard of journalistic ethics to follow. By the 17th century, historians began the practice of citing their sources in [footnotes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Note_(typography) "Note (typography)"). In 1610 when [Galileo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Galileo "Galileo") went on trial, the demand for verifiable news increased.[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politico-6)

During the 18th century publishers of fake news were fined and banned in the Netherlands; one man, Gerard Lodewijk van der Macht, was banned four times by Dutch authorities—and four times he moved and restarted his press.[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-FiveThirtyEight-167) In the American colonies, [Benjamin Franklin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Franklin "Benjamin Franklin") wrote fake news about murderous "scalping" Indians working with King [George III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_III_of_the_United_Kingdom "George III of the United Kingdom") in an effort to sway public opinion in favor of the American Revolution.[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politico-6)

Canards, the successors of the 16th-century [pasquinade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasquinade "Pasquinade"), were sold in Paris on the street for two centuries, starting in the 17th century. In 1793, [Marie Antoinette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marie_Antoinette "Marie Antoinette") was executed in part because of popular hatred engendered by a canard on which her face had been printed.[[166]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Darnton-168)

During the era of slave-owning in the United States, supporters of slavery propagated fake news stories about African Americans, whom white people considered to have lower status.[[167]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-169) Violence occurred in reaction to the spread of some fake news events. In one instance, stories of African Americans spontaneously turning white spread through the south and struck fear into the hearts of many people.[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politico-6)

Rumors and anxieties about slave rebellions were common in Virginia from the beginning of the colonial period, despite the only major uprising occurring in the 19th century. One particular instance of fake news regarding revolts occurred in 1730. The serving governor of Virginia at the time, Governor William Gooch, reported that a slave rebellion had occurred but was effectively put down—although this never happened. After Gooch discovered the falsehood, he ordered slaves found off plantations to be punished, tortured and made prisoners.[[168]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-170)

### 19th century

[![Image 15: b&w drawing of a man with large bat-wings reaching from over his head to mid-thigh](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/6/65/1836_the-great-moon-hoax-new-inhabitants-of-the-moon.png/250px-1836_the-great-moon-hoax-new-inhabitants-of-the-moon.png?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1836_the-great-moon-hoax-new-inhabitants-of-the-moon.png)

A "lunar animal" said to have been discovered by [John Herschel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Herschel "John Herschel") on the [Moon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon "Moon")

From 1800 to 1810, James Cheetham made use of fictional stories to advocate politically against Aaron Burr.[[169]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Nine_letters-171)[[170]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-A_view-172) His stories were often defamatory and he was frequently sued for libel.[[171]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-173)[[172]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-174)[[173]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-175)

One instance of fake news was the [Great Moon Hoax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Moon_Hoax "Great Moon Hoax") of 1835. _[The Sun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Sun\_(New\_York\_City) "The Sun (New York City)")_ newspaper of New York published articles about a real-life astronomer and a made-up colleague who, according to the hoax, had observed bizarre life on the Moon. The fictionalized articles successfully attracted new subscribers, and the penny paper suffered very little backlash after it admitted the next month that the series had been a hoax.[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politico-6)[[174]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-176) Such stories were intended to entertain readers and not to mislead them.[[165]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-FiveThirtyEight-167)

[Yellow journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yellow_journalism "Yellow journalism") peaked in the mid-1890s characterizing the sensationalist journalism that arose in the circulation war between [Joseph Pulitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Pulitzer "Joseph Pulitzer")'s _New York World_ and [William Randolph Hearst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Randolph_Hearst "William Randolph Hearst")'s _New York Journal_. Pulitzer and other yellow journalism publishers goaded the United States into the [Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War "Spanish–American War"), which was precipitated when the [USS _Maine_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Maine_(1890) "USS Maine (1890)") exploded in the harbor of [Havana](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Havana "Havana"), [Cuba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuba "Cuba").[[175]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-177) The term _fake news_ itself was apparently first used in the 1890s during this era of sensationalist news reporting.[[5]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-webster-5)

[![Image 16: two men dressed as the Yellow Kid pushing on opposite sides of oversize building blocks bearing the letters W A R"](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/41/PulitzerHearstWarYellowKids.jpg/250px-PulitzerHearstWarYellowKids.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:PulitzerHearstWarYellowKids.jpg)

[Joseph Pulitzer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Pulitzer "Joseph Pulitzer") and [William Randolph Hearst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Randolph_Hearst "William Randolph Hearst") caricatured as they urged the U.S. into the [Spanish–American War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spanish%E2%80%93American_War "Spanish–American War")

### 20th century

Fake news became popular and spread quickly in the 1900s. Media like newspapers, articles, and magazines were in high demand because of technology.[[176]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-178) Author [Sarah Churchwell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sarah_Churchwell "Sarah Churchwell") shows that when _The New York Times_ reprinted the 1915 speech by Woodrow Wilson that popularized the phrase _[America First](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/America\_First\_(policy) "America First (policy)")_, they also used the subheading "Fake News Condemned" to describe a section of his speech warning against propaganda and misinformation, although Wilson himself had not used the phrase _fake news_.
