In his speech, Wilson warned of a growing problem with news that "turn[s] out to be falsehood", warning the country it "could not afford 'to let the rumors of irresponsible persons and origins get into the United States'" as that would undermine democracy and the principle of a free and accurate press.[[177]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-179)

Following a claim by [CNN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN "CNN") that "Trump was... the first US President to deploy [the term "fake news"] against his opponents",[[178]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-180) Sarah Churchwell's work was cited to claim that "it was Woodrow Wilson who popularized the phrase 'fake news' in 1915" without reference,[[179]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-181) forcing her to counter this claim, saying that "the phrase 'fake news' was very much NOT popularized (or even used) by Wilson. The NY Times used it in passing but it didn't catch on. Trump was the first to popularize it."[[180]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-182)

[![Image 17](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f8/Peace_rumor%2C_New_York._Crowd_at_Times_Square_holding_up_Extras_telling_about_the_signing_of_the_Armistice._The..._-_NARA_-_533477.tif/lossy-page1-250px-Peace_rumor%2C_New_York._Crowd_at_Times_Square_holding_up_Extras_telling_about_the_signing_of_the_Armistice._The..._-_NARA_-_533477.tif.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Peace_rumor,_New_York._Crowd_at_Times_Square_holding_up_Extras_telling_about_the_signing_of_the_Armistice._The..._-_NARA_-_533477.tif)

Residents of [New York City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_City "New York City") celebrate the news of the [Armistice of 11 November 1918](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armistice_of_11_November_1918 "Armistice of 11 November 1918").

During the [First World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_World_War "First World War"), an example of fake news was the [anti-German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-German_sentiment "Anti-German sentiment")[atrocity propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atrocity_propaganda "Atrocity propaganda") regarding an alleged "[German Corpse Factory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/German_Corpse_Factory "German Corpse Factory")" in which the German battlefield dead were supposedly rendered down for fats used to make [nitroglycerine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitroglycerine "Nitroglycerine"), candles, lubricants, [human soap](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soap_made_from_human_corpses "Soap made from human corpses") and boot dubbing. Unfounded rumors regarding such a factory circulated in the [Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I "Allies of World War I") press starting in 1915, and by 1917 the English-language publication _[North China Daily News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North\_China\_Daily\_News "North China Daily News")_ presented these allegations as true at a time when Britain was trying to convince China to join the Allied war effort; this was based on new, allegedly true stories from _[The Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Times "The Times")_ and the _[Daily Mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Daily\_Mail "The Daily Mail")_ that turned out to be forgeries.

These false allegations became known as such after the war, and in the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War "Second World War")[Joseph Goebbels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Goebbels "Joseph Goebbels") used the story in order to [deny the ongoing massacre of Jews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holocaust_denial "Holocaust denial") as British propaganda. According to [Joachim Neander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joachim_Neander "Joachim Neander") and [Randal Marlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randal_Marlin "Randal Marlin"), the story also "encouraged later disbelief" when reports about the Holocaust surfaced after the liberation of [Auschwitz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auschwitz_concentration_camp "Auschwitz concentration camp") and [Dachau concentration camps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dachau_concentration_camp "Dachau concentration camp").[[181]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-BBC_fake_news-183) After [Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adolf_Hitler "Adolf Hitler") and the [Nazi Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Party "Nazi Party") rose to power in Germany in 1933, they established the [Reich Ministry of Public Enlightenment and Propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reich_Ministry_of_Public_Enlightenment_and_Propaganda "Reich Ministry of Public Enlightenment and Propaganda") under the control of Propaganda Minister [Joseph Goebbels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Goebbels "Joseph Goebbels").[[182]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-American_Experience-2006-184) The Nazis used both print and broadcast journalism to promote their agendas, either by obtaining ownership of those media or exerting political influence.[[183]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-185) The expression _[big lie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big\_lie "Big lie")_ (German: _große Lüge_) was coined by Adolf Hitler, when he dictated his 1925 book _[Mein Kampf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mein\_Kampf "Mein Kampf")_. Throughout [World War II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_War_II "World War II"), both the [Axis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Axis_powers "Axis powers") and the [Allies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_II "Allies of World War II") employed fake news in the form of propaganda to persuade the public at home and in enemy countries.[[184]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-186)[[185]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-187) The British [Political Warfare Executive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_Warfare_Executive "Political Warfare Executive") used radio broadcasts and distributed leaflets to discourage German troops.[[182]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-American_Experience-2006-184)

The [Carnegie Endowment for International Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carnegie_Endowment_for_International_Peace "Carnegie Endowment for International Peace") claimed that _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_New\_York\_Times "The New York Times")_ printed fake news "depicting Russia as a socialist paradise".[[186]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-188) During 1932–1933, _The New York Times_ published numerous articles by its Moscow bureau chief, [Walter Duranty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walter_Duranty "Walter Duranty"), who won a [Pulitzer Prize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pulitzer_Prize "Pulitzer Prize") for a series of reports about the Soviet Union.

### 21st century

[Video 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder)[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dictators_-_Kim_Jong-Un_by_RepresentUs.webm "Play video")[](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Dictators_-_Kim_Jong-Un_by_RepresentUs.webm)

Deep fakes

In the 21st century, both the impact of fake news and the use of the term became widespread.[[16]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-NYT-20170623-16)[[187]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-189)

The increasing openness, access and prevalence of the Internet resulted in its growth. New information and stories are published constantly and at a faster rate than ever, often lacking in verification, which may be consumed by anyone with an Internet connection.[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-spotfake-75)[[188]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-190) Fake news has grown from being sent via emails to attacking social media.[[75]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-spotfake-75) Besides referring to made-up stories designed to deceive readers into clicking on links, maximizing traffic and profit, the term has also referred to satirical news, whose purpose is not to mislead but rather to inform viewers and share humorous commentary about real news and the [mainstream media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainstream_media "Mainstream media").[[189]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-191)[[190]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-192) United States examples of satire include the newspaper _[The Onion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Onion "The Onion")_, _[Saturday Night Live](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saturday\_Night\_Live "Saturday Night Live")_'s _[Weekend Update](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Weekend\_Update "Weekend Update")_, and the television shows _[The Daily Show](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Daily\_Show "The Daily Show")_ and _[The Colbert Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Colbert\_Report "The Colbert Report")_.[[191]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-193)[[192]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-194)[[193]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-195)

21st-century fake news is often intended to increase the financial profits of the news outlet. In an interview with [NPR](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NPR "NPR"), Jestin Coler, former CEO of the fake media conglomerate [Disinfomedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_Guardian "Denver Guardian"), told who writes fake news articles, who funds these articles, and why fake news creators create and distribute false information. Coler, who has since left his role as a fake news creator, said that his company employed 20 to 25 writers at a time and made $10,000 to $30,000 monthly from advertisements. Coler began his career in journalism as a magazine salesman before working as a freelance writer. He said he entered the fake news industry to prove to himself and others just how rapidly fake news can spread.[[194]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-196)

Disinfomedia is not the only outlet responsible for the distribution of fake news; Facebook users play a major role in feeding into fake news stories by making sensationalized stories "trend", according to [BuzzFeed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BuzzFeed "BuzzFeed") media editor Craig Silverman, and the individuals behind Google AdSense basically fund fake news websites and their content.[[195]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-197)[Mark Zuckerberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mark_Zuckerberg "Mark Zuckerberg"), CEO of Facebook, said, "I think the idea that fake news on Facebook influenced the election in any way, I think is a pretty crazy idea" and then a few days later he blogged that Facebook was looking for ways to flag fake news stories.[[196]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-CBSNews20161117-198)

Many online pro-[Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") fake news stories have been sourced out of [Veles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Veles,_Macedonia "Veles, Macedonia"), [Macedonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Macedonia "Republic of Macedonia"), where approximately seven different fake news organizations employ hundreds of teenagers to rapidly produce and plagiarize sensationalist stories for different U.S. based companies and parties.[[197]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-199)

Kim LaCapria of the fact checking website [Snopes.com](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snopes.com "Snopes.com") has stated that, in America, fake news is a bipartisan phenomenon, saying that "[t]here has always been a sincerely held yet erroneous belief misinformation is more [red than blue in America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_states_and_blue_states "Red states and blue states"), and that has never been true."[[198]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-200)[Jeff Green](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jeff_Green_(businessman) "Jeff Green (businessman)") of [Trade Desk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_Desk "Trade Desk") agrees the phenomenon affects both sides. Green's company found that affluent and well-educated persons in their 40s and 50s are the primary consumers of fake news. He told [Scott Pelley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scott_Pelley "Scott Pelley") of _[60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60\_Minutes "60 Minutes")_ that this audience tends to live in an "[echo chamber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Echo_chamber_(media) "Echo chamber (media)")" and that these are the people who vote.[[57]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-60Minutes-57)

In 2014, the [Russian Government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Government "Russian Government") used disinformation via networks such as [RT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RT_(TV_network) "RT (TV network)") to create a counter-narrative after Russian-backed Ukrainian rebels shot down [Malaysia Airlines Flight 17](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Malaysia_Airlines_Flight_17 "Malaysia Airlines Flight 17").[[199]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-201) In 2016, [NATO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NATO "NATO") claimed it had seen a significant rise in Russian propaganda and fake news stories since the invasion of Crimea in 2014.[[200]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-202) Fake news stories originating from Russian government officials were also circulated internationally by [Reuters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reuters "Reuters") news agency and published in the most popular news websites in the United States.[[201]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-203)

A 2018 study at Oxford University found that Trump's supporters consumed the "largest volume of 'junk news' on Facebook and Twitter":[[202]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Narayanan_Barash_2/8/2018-204)

> On Twitter, a network of Trump supporters consumes the largest volume of junk news, and junk news is the largest proportion of news links they share," the researchers concluded. On Facebook, the skew was even greater. There, "extreme hard right pages—distinct from Republican pages—share more junk news than all the other audiences put together.[[203]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Hern_2/6/2018-205)

In 2018,[[204]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guess_Nyhan_Reifler_1/9/2018-206) researchers from [Princeton University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University "Princeton University"), [Dartmouth College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dartmouth_College "Dartmouth College") and the [University of Exeter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Exeter "University of Exeter") examined the consumption of fake news during the 2016 U.S. presidential campaign. Their findings showed that Trump supporters and older Americans (over 60) were far more likely to consume fake news than Clinton supporters. Those most likely to visit fake news websites were the 10% of Americans who consumed the most [conservative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatism "Conservatism") information. There was a very large difference (800%) in the consumption of fake news stories as related to total news consumption between Trump supporters (6%) and Clinton supporters (1%).[[204]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guess_Nyhan_Reifler_1/9/2018-206)[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Sarlin_1/14/2018-207)

The study also showed that fake pro-Trump and fake pro-Clinton news stories were read by their supporters, but with a significant difference: Trump supporters consumed far more (40%) than Clinton supporters (15%). Facebook was by far the key "gateway" website where these fake stories were spread and which led people to then go to the fake news websites. [Fact checks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fact_checking "Fact checking") of fake news were rarely seen by consumers,[[204]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guess_Nyhan_Reifler_1/9/2018-206)[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Sarlin_1/14/2018-207) with none of those who saw a fake news story being reached by a related fact check.[[206]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Poynter_1/3/2018-208)

[Brendan Nyhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brendan_Nyhan "Brendan Nyhan"), one of the researchers, emphatically stated in an interview on NBC News: "People got vastly more misinformation from Donald Trump than they did from fake news websites—full stop."[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Sarlin_1/14/2018-207)

> NBC NEWS: "It feels like there's a connection between having an active portion of a party that's prone to seeking false stories and conspiracies and a president who has famously spread conspiracies and false claims. In many ways, demographically and ideologically, the president fits the profile of the fake news users that you're describing." NYHAN: "It's worrisome if fake news websites further weaken the norm against false and misleading information in our politics, which unfortunately has eroded. But it's also important to put the content provided by fake news websites in perspective. People got vastly more misinformation from Donald Trump than they did from fake news websites—full stop."[[205]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Sarlin_1/14/2018-207)

A 2019 study by researchers at [Princeton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princeton_University "Princeton University") and [New York University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_York_University "New York University") found that a person's likelihood of sharing fake-news articles correlated more strongly with age than it did education, sex, or political views. 11% of users older than 65 shared an article consistent with the study's definition of fake news. Just 3% of users ages 18 to 29 did the same.[[207]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-209)

Another issue in mainstream media is the usage of the [filter bubble](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filter_bubble "Filter bubble"), a "bubble" that has been created that gives the viewer, on social media platforms, a specific piece of the information knowing they will like it. Thus creating fake news and biased news because only half the story is being shared, the portion the viewer liked. "In 1996, Nicolas Negroponte predicted a world where information technologies become increasingly customizable."[[208]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-210)

In a study conducted by [Ofcom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ofcom "Ofcom") from September 29 to November 28, 2025, and published on April 2, 2026, 56% of social media users stated that had seen misleading or false news in the last year.[[209]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-211)

## Special topics

### Deepfakes and shallowfakes

See also: [Deepfakes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deepfakes "Deepfakes")

**Deepfakes** (a [portmanteau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portmanteau "Portmanteau") of _[deep learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deep\_learning "Deep learning")_ and _fake_) are [synthetic media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic_media "Synthetic media") (AI-generated media)[[210]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-212) in which a person in an existing image or video is replaced with someone else's likeness.[[211]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Waldrop-213)

Because a picture often has a greater impact than the corresponding words, deepfakes—which leverage powerful techniques from [machine learning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Machine_learning "Machine learning") and [artificial intelligence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_intelligence "Artificial intelligence") to manipulate or generate visual and audio content—have a particularly high potential to deceive.[[212]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Kietzmann-2020-214) The main machine-learning methods used to create deepfakes are based on deep learning and involve training generative [neural network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Artificial_neural_network "Artificial neural network") architectures, such as [autoencoders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Autoencoder "Autoencoder")[[212]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Kietzmann-2020-214) or [generative adversarial networks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Generative_adversarial_network "Generative adversarial network") (GANs).[[213]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Schwartz-215)

Deepfakes have garnered widespread attention for their uses in creating fake news (notably political), but also [child sexual abuse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Child_sexual_abuse "Child sexual abuse") material, [celebrity pornographic videos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Celebrity_sex_tape "Celebrity sex tape"), [revenge porn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Revenge_porn "Revenge porn"), [hoaxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoax "Hoax"), bullying, and [financial fraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Accounting_scandals "Accounting scandals").[[214]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-HighSnobiety2018-216)[[215]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-217)[[216]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-218) This has elicited responses from both industry and government to detect and limit their use.[[217]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Ghoshal-2018-219)[[218]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Santos_Calderon-2019-220)

Deepfakes generally require specialist software or knowledge, but much the same effect can be achieved quickly by anyone using standard video editing software on most modern computers. These videos (termed _shallowfakes_) have obvious flaws, but nevertheless can still be widely believed as real, or at least have entertainment value that reinforces beliefs. One of the first to go viral, "The Hillary Song", watched over 3 million times, shows Hillary Clinton being humiliated on stage by [The Rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwayne_Johnson "Dwayne Johnson"), a former wrestling champion. The surprised creator (who hates all politicians), pasted images of Clinton into a genuine video of The Rock humiliating a wrestling official.[[219]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-221)

### Bots on social media

In the mid-1990s, [Nicolas Negroponte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nicholas_Negroponte "Nicholas Negroponte") anticipated a world where news through technology become progressively personalized. In his 1996 book _Being Digital_ he predicted a digital life where news consumption becomes an extremely personalized experience and newspapers adapted content to reader preferences. This prediction has since been reflected in modern-day news and social media feeds.[[220]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-222)

Bots have the potential to increase the spread of fake news, as they use algorithms to decide what articles and information specific users like, without taking into account the authenticity of an article. Bots mass-produce and spread articles, regardless of the credibility of the sources, allowing them to play an essential role in the mass spread of fake news, as bots are capable of creating fake accounts and personalities on the web that are then gaining followers, recognition, and authority.[[221]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-223)[[222]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:4-224) The "2016 Bot Traffic Report" estimated that approximately 30% of the internet's traffic is from malicious bots.[[222]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:4-224)

In the 21st century, the capacity to mislead was enhanced by the widespread use of social media. For example, one 21st century website that enabled fake news' proliferation was the [Facebook newsfeed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Facebook_features "List of Facebook features").[[223]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-isaac-225)[[224]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-FinancialTimes-226) In late 2016 fake news gained notoriety following the uptick in news content by this means, and its prevalence on the [micro-blogging](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microblogging "Microblogging") site [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter").[[11]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guardian11.11.16-11)[[6]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politico-6) In the United States, 62% of Americans use social media to receive news.[[42]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-pew2-42) Many people use their Facebook News Feed to get news, despite Facebook not being considered a news site.[[225]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-227) According to Craig McClain, over 66% of Facebook users obtain news from the site.[[226]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-228) This, in combination with increased political polarization and [filter bubbles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filter_bubble "Filter bubble"), led to a tendency for readers to mainly read headlines.[[227]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-229)

Numerous individuals and news outlets have stated that fake news may have influenced the outcome of the [2016 American Presidential Election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_American_Presidential_Election "2016 American Presidential Election").[[228]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-230)[[229]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-231) Fake news saw higher sharing on Facebook than legitimate news stories,[[13]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Silverman_11/16/2016-13)[[230]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-232)[[231]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-PCWorld-233)[[232]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-234) which analysts explained was because fake news often panders to expectations or is otherwise more exciting than legitimate news.[[231]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-PCWorld-233)[[233]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-LATimes-235)[Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook") itself initially denied this characterization.[[224]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-FinancialTimes-226)[[223]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-isaac-225) A [Pew Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pew_Research "Pew Research") poll conducted in December 2016 found that 64% of U.S. adults believed completely made-up news had caused "a great deal of confusion" about the basic facts of current events, while 24% claimed it had caused "some confusion" and 11% said it had caused "not much or no confusion".[[38]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-pew1-38) Additionally, 23% of those polled admitted they had personally shared fake news, whether knowingly or not. Researchers from [Stanford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stanford "Stanford") assessed that only 8% of readers of fake news recalled and believed in the content they were reading, though the same share of readers also recalled and believed in "placebos"—stories they did not actually read, but that were produced by the authors of the study. In comparison, over 50% of the participants recalled reading and believed in true news stories.[[18]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Allcott-2017-18)

By August 2017 Facebook stopped using the term _fake news_ and used _false news_ in its place instead. Will Oremus of _[Slate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slate\_(magazine) "Slate (magazine)")_ wrote that because supporters of [U.S. President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._President "U.S. President")[Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") had redefined the word _fake news_ to refer to mainstream media opposed to them, "it makes sense for Facebook—and others—to cede the term to the right-wing trolls who have claimed it as their own."[[234]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-236)

Research from [Northwestern University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_University "Northwestern University") concluded that 30% of all fake news traffic, as opposed to only 8% of real news traffic, could be linked back to Facebook. The research concluded fake news consumers do not exist in a filter bubble; many of them also consume real news from established news sources. The fake news audience is only 10 percent of the real news audience, and most fake news consumers spent a relatively similar amount of time on fake news compared with real news consumers—with the exception of [Drudge Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drudge_Report "Drudge Report") readers, who spent more than 11 times longer reading the website than other users.[[235]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-ColumbiaJReview-237)

In the wake of western events, China's Ren Xianling of the [Cyberspace Administration of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyberspace_Administration_of_China "Cyberspace Administration of China") suggested a "reward and punish" system be implemented to avoid fake news.[[236]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-238)

### Internet trolls

See also: [Internet trolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_trolls "Internet trolls")

In [Internet slang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_slang "Internet slang"), a [troll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_troll "Internet troll") is a person who sows discord on the [Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet "Internet") by starting arguments or upsetting people, by posting inflammatory, [extraneous](https://en.wiktionary.org/wiki/Extraneous#Adjective "wikt:Extraneous"), or [off-topic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Off-topic "Off-topic") messages in an online community (such as a [newsgroup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newsgroup "Newsgroup"), forum, [chat room](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chat_room "Chat room"), or blog) with the intent of provoking readers into an [emotional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emotion "Emotion") response or off-topic discussion, often for the troll's amusement. Internet trolls also feed on attention.[[237]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-time1-239)

The idea of Internet trolls gained popularity in the 1990s, though its meaning shifted in 2011. Whereas it once denoted provocation, it is a term now widely used to signify the abuse and misuse of the Internet. Trolling comes in various forms, and can be dissected into abuse trolling, entertainment trolling, classical trolling, flame trolling, anonymous trolling, and kudos trolling. It is closely linked to fake news, as Internet trolls are now largely interpreted as perpetrators of false information, information that can often be passed along unwittingly by reporters and the public alike.[[238]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-240)[[239]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-241)

When interacting with each other, trolls often share misleading information that contributes to the fake news circulated on sites like [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter") and [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook").[[237]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-time1-239) In the 2016 American election, Russia paid over 1,000 Internet trolls to circulate fake news and disinformation about [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton "Hillary Clinton"); they also created social media accounts that resembled voters in important swing states, spreading influential political standpoints.[[240]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-242)[[241]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-243) In February 2019, [Glenn Greenwald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glenn_Greenwald "Glenn Greenwald") wrote that cybersecurity company New Knowledge "was caught just six weeks ago engaging in a massive scam to create fictitious Russian troll accounts on Facebook and Twitter in order to claim that the Kremlin was working to defeat Democratic Senate nominee [Doug Jones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doug_Jones_(politician) "Doug Jones (politician)") in [Alabama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alabama "Alabama")."[[242]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-244)

### Fake news hoaxes

[Paul Horner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Horner "Paul Horner") is perhaps the best known example of a person who deliberately creates fake news for a purpose. He has been referred to as a "hoax artist" by the [Associated Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Associated_Press "Associated Press") and the _[Chicago Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chicago\_Tribune "Chicago Tribune")_.[[243]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-245)_[The Huffington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Huffington\_Post "The Huffington Post")_ called Horner a "performance artist".[[244]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-246)

Horner was behind several widespread hoaxes such as: (1) that the graffiti artist [Banksy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Banksy "Banksy") had been arrested;[[245]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-247)[[246]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-248) and (2) that he had an "enormous impact" on the [2016 U.S. presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election "2016 United States presidential election"), according to [CBS News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBS_News "CBS News").[[247]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-249) These stories consistently appeared in [Google](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Search "Google Search")'s top news search results, were shared widely on [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook"), were taken seriously and shared by third parties such as [Trump presidential campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump_presidential_campaign,_2016 "Donald Trump presidential campaign, 2016") manager [Corey Lewandowski](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corey_Lewandowski "Corey Lewandowski"), [Eric Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eric_Trump "Eric Trump"), [ABC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ABC_News_(United_States) "ABC News (United States)") and the [Fox News Channel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox_News_Channel "Fox News Channel").[[248]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-politfact-250)[[249]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-251)[[250]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-252) Horner later claimed that the intention was "to make Trump's supporters look like idiots for sharing my stories".[[251]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-bratu-253)

In a November 2016 interview with _[The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Washington\_Post "The Washington Post")_, Horner expressed regret for the role his fake news stories played in the election and surprise at how gullible people were in treating his stories as news.[[252]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-hedegaard-254)[[253]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-255)[[254]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-256)[[255]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-257) In February 2017 Horner said,

> I truly regret my comment that I think Donald Trump is in the White House because of me. I know all I did was attack him and his supporters and got people not to vote for him. When I said that comment it was because I was confused how this evil man got elected President and I thought maybe instead of hurting his campaign, maybe I had helped it. My intention was to get his supporters NOT to vote for him and I know for a fact that I accomplished that goal. The far right, a lot of the Bible thumpers and [alt-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alt-right "Alt-right") were going to vote him regardless, but I know I swayed so many that were on the fence.[[256]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-258)

In 2017, Horner stated that a fake story of his about a rape festival in India helped generate over $250,000 in donations to GiveIndia, a site that helps rape victims in India.[[257]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-259)[[258]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-260)[[259]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-261) Horner said he dislikes being grouped with people who write fake news solely to be misleading. "They just write it just to write fake news, like there's no purpose, there's no satire, there's nothing clever.

## By country

### Europe

#### Austria

Politicians in Austria dealt with the impact of fake news and its spread on social media after the 2016 presidential campaign in the country. In December 2016, a court in Austria issued an injunction on Facebook Europe, mandating it block negative postings related to [Eva Glawischnig-Piesczek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eva_Glawischnig-Piesczek "Eva Glawischnig-Piesczek"), [Austrian Green Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austrian_Green_Party "Austrian Green Party") Chairwoman. According to _The Washington Post_ the postings to Facebook about her "appeared to have been spread via a fake profile" and directed derogatory epithets towards the Austrian politician.[[260]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Kirchner-2016-262) The derogatory postings were likely created by the identical fake profile that had previously been utilized to attack [Alexander van der Bellen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_van_der_Bellen "Alexander van der Bellen"), who won the election for [President of Austria](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_Austria "President of Austria").[[260]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Kirchner-2016-262)

#### Belgium

Main article: [Flemish Secession hoax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flemish_Secession_hoax "Flemish Secession hoax")

In 2006, French-speaking broadcaster [RTBF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RTBF "RTBF") showed a fictional breaking special news report that Belgium's [Flemish Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flemish_Region "Flemish Region") had proclaimed independence. Staged footage of the [royal family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_royal_family "Belgian royal family") evacuating and the Belgian flag being lowered from a pole were made to add credence to the report. It was not until 30 minutes into the report that a sign stating "Fiction" appeared on screen. The RTBF journalist that created the hoax said the purpose was to demonstrate the magnitude of the country's situation and if a partition of Belgium was to really happen.[[261]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-263)

#### Czech Republic

See also: [Centre Against Terrorism and Hybrid Threats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_Against_Terrorism_and_Hybrid_Threats "Centre Against Terrorism and Hybrid Threats")

Fake news outlets in the Czech Republic redistribute news in Czech and English originally produced by Russian sources. Czech president [Miloš Zeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milo%C5%A1_Zeman "Miloš Zeman") has been supporting media outlets accused of spreading fake news.[[262]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-264)

The _Centre Against Terrorism and Hybrid Threats_ (CTHH) is unit of the [Ministry of the Interior of the Czech Republic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_the_Interior_(Czech_Republic) "Ministry of the Interior (Czech Republic)") primarily aimed at countering disinformation, fake news, [hoaxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hoax "Hoax") and foreign [propaganda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Propaganda "Propaganda"). The CTHH started operations on January 1, 2017. The CTHH has been criticized by Czech President [Miloš Zeman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milo%C5%A1_Zeman "Miloš Zeman"), who said: "We don't need censorship. We don't need thought police. We don't need a new agency for press and information as long as we want to live in a free and democratic society."[[263]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-265)

In 2017 media activists started a website, Konspiratori.cz, maintaining a list of conspiracy and fake news outlets in Czech.[[264]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-266)

#### European Union

In 2018 the [European Commission](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Commission "European Commission") introduced a first voluntary [code of practice on disinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU_Code_of_Practice_on_Disinformation "EU Code of Practice on Disinformation"). In 2022 this will become a strengthen co-regulation scheme, with responsibility shared between the regulators and companies signatory to the code. It will complement the earlier [Digital Services Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digital_Services_Act "Digital Services Act") agreed on by the 27-country European Union, which already includes a section on combating disinformation.[[265]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-267)[[266]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-268)[_[needs update](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual\_of\_Style/Dates\_and\_numbers#Chronological\_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")_]

#### Finland

Officials from 11 countries met in [Helsinki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Helsinki "Helsinki") in November 2016 and planned the formation of a center to combat disinformation cyber-warfare, which includes the spread of fake news on social media. The center is planned to be located in Helsinki and combine efforts from 10 countries, including Sweden, Germany, Finland and the U.S. [Juha Sipilä](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juha_Sipil%C3%A4 "Juha Sipilä"), [Prime Minister of Finland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Finland "Prime Minister of Finland") from 2015 to 2019, planned to address the topic of the center in Spring 2017 with a motion before [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Finland "Parliament of Finland").[_[needs update](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual\_of\_Style/Dates\_and\_numbers#Chronological\_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")_]

Deputy Secretary of State for EU Affairs Jori Arvonen said cyber-warfare, such as hybrid cyber-warfare intrusions into Finland from Russia and the [Islamic State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_State_of_Iraq_and_the_Levant "Islamic State of Iraq and the Levant"), became an increasing problem in 2016. Arvonen cited examples including online fake news, disinformation, and the "[little green men](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Little_green_men_(Russo-Ukrainian_War) "Little green men (Russo-Ukrainian War)")" of the [Russo-Ukrainian War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Ukrainian_War "Russo-Ukrainian War").[[267]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-269)

#### France

During the ten-year period preceding 2016, France was witness to an increase in popularity of [far-right](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Far-right "Far-right") alternative news sources called the _fachosphere_ (_facho_ referring to [fascist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascist "Fascist")); known as the [extreme right on the Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_right_on_the_Internet "Extreme right on the Internet").[[268]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guardian12.2.16-270) According to sociologist Antoine Bevort, citing data from [Alexa Internet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexa_Internet "Alexa Internet") rankings, the most consulted political websites in France in 2016 included _[Égalité et Réconciliation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%C3%89galit%C3%A9\_et\_R%C3%A9conciliation "Égalité et Réconciliation")_, _[François Desouche](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois\_Desouche?action=edit&redlink=1 "François Desouche (page does not exist)")[[fr](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fran%C3%A7ois%20Desouche "fr:François Desouche")]_, and _Les Moutons Enragés_.[[269]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-271)[[270]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-272) These sites increased skepticism towards [mainstream media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainstream_media "Mainstream media") from both left and right perspectives.

In September 2016, the country faced controversy regarding fake websites providing false information about [abortion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abortion "Abortion"). The [National Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Assembly_(France) "National Assembly (France)") moved forward with intentions to ban such fake sites. [Laurence Rossignol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurence_Rossignol "Laurence Rossignol"), women's minister for France, informed parliament though the fake sites look neutral, in actuality their intentions were specifically targeted to give women fake information.

2017 presidential election. France saw an uptick in amounts of disinformation and propaganda, primarily in the midst of election cycles. A study looking at the diffusion of political news during the 2017 presidential election cycle suggests that one in four links shared in social media comes from sources that actively contest traditional media narratives.[[271]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-273)[Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook") corporate deleted 30,000 Facebook accounts in France associated with fake political information.[[272]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-indepfr-274)

In April 2017, [Emmanuel Macron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmanuel_Macron "Emmanuel Macron")'s presidential campaign was attacked by the fake news articles more than the campaigns of conservative candidate [Marine Le Pen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marine_Le_Pen "Marine Le Pen") and socialist candidate [Benoît Hamon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beno%C3%AEt_Hamon "Benoît Hamon").[[273]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-275) One of the fake articles even announced that Le Pen won the presidency before the people of France had even voted.[[272]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-indepfr-274) Macron's professional and private emails, as well as memos, contracts and accounting documents were posted on a file-sharing website. The leaked documents were mixed with fake ones in social media in an attempt to sway the upcoming presidential election.[[274]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-276) Macron said he would combat fake news of the sort that had been spread during his election campaign.[[275]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-277)

Initially, the leak was attributed to [APT28](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fancy_Bear "Fancy Bear"), a group tied to Russia's GRU military intelligence directorate.[[276]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-278) However, the head of the French cyber-security agency, [ANSSI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agence_nationale_de_la_s%C3%A9curit%C3%A9_des_syst%C3%A8mes_d'information "Agence nationale de la sécurité des systèmes d'information"), later said that there was no evidence that the hack leading to the leaks had anything to do with Russia, saying that the attack was so simple, that "we can imagine that it was a person who did this alone. They could be in any country."[[277]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-279)

#### Germany

[German Chancellor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chancellor_of_Germany "Chancellor of Germany")[Angela Merkel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Angela_Merkel "Angela Merkel") lamented the problem of fraudulent news reports in a November 2016 speech, days after announcing her campaign for a fourth term as leader of her country. In a speech to the German parliament, Merkel was critical of such fake sites, saying they harmed political discussion. Merkel called attention to the need of government to deal with [Internet trolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_troll "Internet troll"), bots, and fake news websites. She warned that such fraudulent news websites were a force increasing the power of [populist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Populism "Populism") extremism. Merkel called fraudulent news a growing phenomenon that might need to be regulated in the future. The head of Germany's foreign intelligence agency [Federal Intelligence Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Intelligence_Service_(Germany) "Federal Intelligence Service (Germany)"), [Bruno Kahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruno_Kahl "Bruno Kahl"), warned of the potential for [cyberattacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyberattacks "Cyberattacks") by Russia in the [2017 German election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_German_federal_election "2017 German federal election"). He said the cyberattacks would take the form of the intentional spread of disinformation. Kahl said the goal is to increase chaos in political debates. Germany's domestic intelligence agency [Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Federal_Office_for_the_Protection_of_the_Constitution "Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution") Chief, [Hans-Georg Maassen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hans-Georg_Maassen "Hans-Georg Maassen"), said sabotage by Russian intelligence was a present threat to German [information security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_security "Information security").[[278]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-280) German government officials and security experts later said there was no Russian interference during the 2017 German federal election.[[279]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-281) The German term _Lügenpresse_, or [lying press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lying_press "Lying press"), has been used since the 19th century and specifically during World War One as a strategy to attack news spread by political opponents in the 19th and 20th centuries.[[280]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-282)

The award-winning German journalist [Claas Relotius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Claas_Relotius "Claas Relotius") resigned from _[Der Spiegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Der\_Spiegel "Der Spiegel")_ in 2018 after admitting numerous instances of [journalistic fraud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journalistic_fraud "Journalistic fraud").[[281]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-283)

In early April 2020, Berlin politician [Andreas Geisel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andreas_Geisel "Andreas Geisel") alleged that a shipment of 200,000 N95 masks that it had ordered from American producer [3M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/3M "3M")'s China facility were intercepted in [Bangkok](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bangkok "Bangkok") and diverted to the United States during the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic "COVID-19 pandemic"). [Berlin police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berlin_Police "Berlin Police") president [Barbara Slowik](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Slowik?action=edit&redlink=1 "Barbara Slowik (page does not exist)")[[de](https://de.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara%20Slowik "de:Barbara Slowik")] stated that she believed "this is related to the US government's export ban."[[282]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-284) However, Berlin police confirmed that the shipment was not seized by U.S. authorities, but was said to have simply been bought at a better price, widely believed to be from a German dealer or China.[[283]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-GWFWD-285)[[284]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-286) This revelation outraged the Berlin opposition, whose [CDU](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christian_Democratic_Union_of_Germany "Christian Democratic Union of Germany") parliamentary group leader Burkard Dregger accused Geisel of "deliberately misleading Berliners" in order "to cover up its own inability to obtain protective equipment". [FDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_Democratic_Party_(Germany) "Free Democratic Party (Germany)") interior expert Marcel Luthe said "Big names in international politics like Berlin's senator Geisel are blaming others and telling US piracy to serve anti-American clichés."[[283]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-GWFWD-285)_[Politico Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politico\_Europe "Politico Europe")_ reported that "the Berliners are taking a page straight out of the Trump playbook and not letting facts get in the way of a good story."[[285]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-287)

#### Hungary

Hungary's illiberal and populist prime minister [Viktor Orbán](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Orb%C3%A1n "Viktor Orbán") has cast [George Soros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Soros "George Soros"), financier and philanthropist, a Hungarian-born Holocaust survivor, as the mastermind of a plot to undermine the country's sovereignty, replace native Hungarians with immigrants and destroy traditional values. This propaganda technique, together with [anti-Semitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antisemitism "Antisemitism") still present in the country, seems to appeal to his right-wing voters, as it mobilizes them by seeding fear in society, creating an enemy image and enabling Orbán to present himself as the protector of the nation from the illusion of this enemy.[[286]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-288)[[287]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-289)

#### Italy

Journalists must be registered with the _Ordine Dei Giornalisti_ (ODG) ('Order of Journalists') and respect its disciplinary and training obligations, to guarantee "correct and truthful information, intended as right of individuals and of the community".[[288]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-290)[[289]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-291)

Under certain circumstances, spreading fake news may constitute a criminal offence under the Italian penal code.[[290]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-292)[[291]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-293)[[292]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-294)

Since 2018 it is possible to report fake news directly on the [Polizia di Stato](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polizia_di_Stato "Polizia di Stato") website.[[293]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-295)[[294]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-296)

The phenomenon is monitored by the [DIS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dipartimento_delle_Informazioni_per_la_Sicurezza "Dipartimento delle Informazioni per la Sicurezza"), supported by [AISE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AISE "AISE") and [AISI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agenzia_Informazioni_e_Sicurezza_Interna "Agenzia Informazioni e Sicurezza Interna").[[295]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-297)[[296]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-298)[[297]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-299)

#### Malta

In response to the growing concern over the spread of disinformation, Malta has introduced legal provisions to address the issue within its [Criminal Code.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Criminal_code "Criminal code").[[298]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-300) Article 82 of Malta's Criminal Code specifically targets the malicious dissemination of fake news. The law stipulates that anyone who deliberately spreads false information likely to alarm the public, disturb public order, or create commotion among certain classes of the public can be sentenced to [imprisonment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Imprisonment "Imprisonment") for a term of one to three months. If the dissemination of false news results in a [disturbance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attention_deficit_hyperactivity_disorder "Attention deficit hyperactivity disorder"), the penalty increases to imprisonment for a term of one to six months, along with a possible fine of up to €1,000.[[299]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-301)

In February and March 2024, the [Times of Malta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Times_of_Malta "Times of Malta") published a series of articles alleging that [Papaya Ltd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papaya_Ltd?action=edit&redlink=1 "Papaya Ltd (page does not exist)") was involved in money laundering activities and had criminal connections with [Russian organized crime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_mafia "Russian mafia").[[300]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-302) These allegations led to public alarm and concern. However, subsequent investigations by British journalists from [Western Morning News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Morning_News "Western Morning News")[[301]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-303) and Financial Monthly[[302]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-304) revealed that the accusations were baseless. It was found that Papaya Ltd. had been conducting routine inspections as part of its anti-money laundering ([AML](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti%E2%80%93money_laundering "Anti–money laundering")) procedures due to suspicious activities by one of its clients, which turned out to be linked to the financial [pyramid scheme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pyramid_scheme "Pyramid scheme"). The inspections were not targeted at Papaya Ltd. itself but were part of a broader investigation. These investigative reports clarified that no governmental authorities had made any allegations against Papaya Ltd., and the initial reports by the Times of Malta were determined to be false.

#### Netherlands

In March 2018, the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union")'s [East StratCom Task Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/East_StratCom_Task_Force "East StratCom Task Force") compiled a list dubbed a "hall of shame" of articles with suspected [Kremlin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moscow_Kremlin "Moscow Kremlin") attempts to influence political decisions.[[303]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Birnbaum-2018-305) However, controversy arose when three Dutch media outlets claimed they had been wrongfully singled out because of quotes attributed to people with non-mainstream views.[[303]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Birnbaum-2018-305) The news outlets included [ThePostOnline](https://nl.wikipedia.org/wiki/ThePostOnline "nl:ThePostOnline"), _[GeenStijl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GeenStijl "GeenStijl")_, and _[De Gelderlander](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De\_Gelderlander "De Gelderlander")_.[[303]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Birnbaum-2018-305) All three were flagged for publishing articles critical of [Ukrainian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine "Ukraine") policies, and none received any forewarning or opportunity to appeal beforehand.[[303]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Birnbaum-2018-305) This incident has contributed to the growing issue of what defines news as fake, and how freedoms of press and speech can be protected during attempts to curb the spread of false news.

#### Poland

Polish historian Jerzy Targalski noted fake news websites had infiltrated Poland through [anti-establishment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-establishment "Anti-establishment") and [right-wing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Right-wing "Right-wing") sources that copied content from _[Russia Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia\_Today "Russia Today")_. Targalski observed there existed about 20 specific fake news websites in Poland that spread Russian disinformation in the form of fake news. One example cited was fake news that [Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukraine "Ukraine") announced the Polish city of [Przemyśl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Przemy%C5%9Bl "Przemyśl") as occupied Polish land.[[304]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-306)

[Poland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poland "Poland")'s anti-EU [Law and Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_and_Justice_(Poland) "Law and Justice (Poland)") (PiS) government has been accused of spreading "illiberal disinformation" to undermine public confidence in the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union").[[305]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-fp-307) Maria Snegovaya of Columbia University said: "The true origins of this phenomenon are local. The policies of Fidesz and Law and Justice have a lot in common with Putin's own policies."[[305]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-fp-307)

Some mainstream outlets have long been accused of fabricating half-true or outright false information. In 2010 one popular TV station, TVN, attributed to Jarosław Kaczyński (then an opposition leader) the quote that "there will be times when true Poles will come to power".[[306]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-jktvn-308) However, Kaczyński has never uttered those words in the commented speech.

#### Romania

On March 16, 2020, [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romania "Romania") President [Klaus Iohannis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Klaus_Iohannis "Klaus Iohannis") signed an emergency decree, giving authorities the power to remove, report or close websites spreading "fake news" about the COVID-19 pandemic, with no opportunity to appeal.[[307]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-309)[[308]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-310)

#### Serbia

In 2018, the [International Research & Exchanges Board](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Research_&_Exchanges_Board "International Research & Exchanges Board") described the situation in Serbian media as the worst in recent history and stated that the [Media Sustainability Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_Sustainability_Index "Media Sustainability Index") has dropped due to the most polarized media in almost 20 years, an increase in fake news and editorial pressure on media.[[309]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-IREX-311) According to Serbian investigative journalism portal _Crime and Corruption Reporting Network_, more than 700 fake news articles were published on the front pages of pro-government tabloids in 2018.[[310]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-312)[[311]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-KRIK-313) Many of them were about alleged attacks on president [Aleksandar Vučić](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aleksandar_Vu%C4%8Di%C4%87 "Aleksandar Vučić") and [coup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coup_d'%C3%A9tat "Coup d'état") attempts, as well as messages of support to him by [Vladimir Putin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vladimir_Putin "Vladimir Putin").[[311]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-KRIK-313) The best-selling newspaper in Serbia is the pro-government tabloid _[Informer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Informer\_(newspaper) "Informer (newspaper)")_, which most often presents Vučić as a powerful person under constant attack, and also features [anti-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euroscepticism "Euroscepticism") content and [pro-war rhetoric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pro-war_rhetoric "Pro-war rhetoric").[[312]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-JovanovicSrdjanMladenov-314)[[313]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-315)[[314]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-316) Since Vučić's party came to power, Serbia has seen a surge of [Internet trolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_trolls "Internet trolls") and pages on social networks praising the government and attacking its critics, free media and the opposition in general.[[315]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Breitbart-317) It includes a handful of dedicated employee-run fake accounts as well as the Facebook page associated with a Serbian franchise of the far-right [Breitbart News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breitbart_News "Breitbart News") website, which has disputed accuracy.[[315]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Breitbart-317)[[316]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-trolls-318)

#### Spain

Fake news in Spain has become much more prevalent in the 2010s, but has been prominent throughout Spain's history. The United States government published a fake article in regards to the purchase of the [Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippines "Philippines") from Spain, which they had already purchased.[[317]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-319) Despite this, the topic of fake news has traditionally not been given much attention in Spain until the newspaper _[El País](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El\_Pa%C3%ADs "El País")_ launched a blog dedicated strictly to truthful news, entitled _Hechos_, meaning 'facts'. David Alandete, the managing editor of _El País_, stated that many people misinterpret fake news as real because the sites "have similar names, typography, layouts and are deliberately confusing".[[318]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Digiday-2017-320) Alandete made it the new mission of _El País_ "to respond to fake news".[[319]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-321) María Ramírez of [Univision Communications](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Univision_Communications "Univision Communications") has stated that much of the political fake news circulating in Spain is due to the lack of investigative journalism on the topics. Most recently _El País_ has created a fact-checking position for five employees, to try and debunk the fake news released.[[318]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Digiday-2017-320)

#### Sweden

The [Swedish Security Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Security_Service "Swedish Security Service") issued a report in 2015 identifying propaganda from Russia infiltrating Sweden with the objective to amplify pro-Russian propaganda and inflame societal conflicts. The [Swedish Civil Contingencies Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Civil_Contingencies_Agency "Swedish Civil Contingencies Agency") (MSB), part of the [Ministry of Defence of Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_(Sweden) "Ministry of Defence (Sweden)"), identified fake news reports targeting [Sweden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweden "Sweden") in 2016 that originated from Russia. Swedish Civil Contingencies Agency official Mikael Tofvesson stated a pattern emerged where views critical of Sweden were constantly repeated. _[The Local](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Local "The Local")_ identified these tactics as a form of [psychological warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Psychological_warfare "Psychological warfare"). The newspaper reported the MSB identified _[Russia Today](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia\_Today "Russia Today")_ and _[Sputnik News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sputnik\_News "Sputnik News")_ as significant fake news purveyors. As a result of growth in this propaganda in Sweden, the MSB planned to hire six additional security officials to fight back against the campaign of fraudulent information.[[320]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-322)

According to the [Oxford Internet Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oxford_Internet_Institute "Oxford Internet Institute"), eight of the top 10 "junk news" sources during the [2018 Swedish general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Swedish_general_election "2018 Swedish general election") campaign were Swedish, and "Russian sources comprised less than 1% of the total number of URLs shared in the data sample."[[321]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-323)

#### Ukraine

Since [Euromaidan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euromaidan "Euromaidan") and the beginning of the [Russo-Ukrainian war](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russo-Ukrainian_war "Russo-Ukrainian war") in 2014, the Ukrainian media circulated several fake news stories and misleading images, including a photograph of a dead rebel with a [Photoshopped](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Photoshopped "Photoshopped") tattoo which allegedly indicated that he belonged to Russian Special Forces[[322]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-324) and the threat of a Russian nuclear attack against Ukrainian troops.[[323]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-BlackJohns-325)[_[page needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing\_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_] The recurring theme of these fake news stories was that Russia was solely to blame for the war.[[323]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-BlackJohns-325)[_[page needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing\_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")_]

In 2015 the [Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organization_for_Security_and_Co-operation_in_Europe "Organization for Security and Co-operation in Europe") published a report criticizing Russian disinformation campaigns to disrupt relations between Europe and Ukraine after the ouster of [Viktor Yanukovych](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Viktor_Yanukovych "Viktor Yanukovych"). According to _[Deutsche Welle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche\_Welle "Deutsche Welle")_, similar tactics were used by fake news websites during the U.S. elections. A website, [StopFake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/StopFake "StopFake"), was created by Ukrainian activists in 2014 to debunk fake news in Ukraine, including [media portrayal of the Ukrainian crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_portrayal_of_the_Ukrainian_crisis "Media portrayal of the Ukrainian crisis").[[324]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-326)

On May 29, 2018, the Ukrainian media and state officials announced that the Russian journalist [Arkady Babchenko](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arkady_Babchenko "Arkady Babchenko") was assassinated in his apartment in Kyiv. Later, Babchenko appeared to be alive, and the [Security Service of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Security_Service_of_Ukraine "Security Service of Ukraine") claimed that the staged assassination was needed to arrest a person who allegedly was planning a real assassination. [Alexander Baunov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alexander_Baunov "Alexander Baunov"), writing for Carnegie.ru, mentioned that the staged assassination of Babchenko was the first instance of fake news delivered directly by the highest officials of a state.[[325]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-327)

#### United Kingdom

Under King [Edward I of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edward_I_of_England "Edward I of England") (r.1272–1307) "a statute was passed which made it a grave offence to devise or tell any false news of prelates, dukes, earls, barons, or nobles of the realm."[[326]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-328)

In 1702 [Queen Anne of England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anne,_Queen_of_Great_Britain "Anne, Queen of Great Britain") issued a proclamation "for restraining the spreading false news, and printing and publishing of irreligious and seditious papers and libels".[[327]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-329)

On December 8, 2016, [Chief of the Secret Intelligence Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chief_of_the_Secret_Intelligence_Service "Chief of the Secret Intelligence Service") (MI6) [Alex Younger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Younger "Alex Younger") delivered a speech to journalists at the [MI6 headquarters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SIS_Building "SIS Building") where he called fake news and propaganda damaging to democracy. Younger said the mission of MI6 was to combat propaganda and fake news in order to deliver to his government a strategic advantage in the [information-warfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_warfare "Information warfare") arena, and to assist other nations including Europe. He called such methods of fake-news propaganda online a "fundamental threat to our sovereignty". Younger said all nations that hold democratic values should feel the same worry over fake news.[[328]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-330)

However, definitions of _fake news_ have been controversial in the UK.[[329]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-331) Dr Claire Wardle advised some UK Members of Parliament against using the term in certain circumstances "when describing the complexity of information disorder", as the term _fake news_ is "woefully inadequate":

> Neither the words 'fake' nor 'news' effectively capture this polluted information ecosystem. Much of the content used as examples in debates on this topic are not fake, they are genuine but used out of context or manipulated. Similarly, to understand the entire ecosystem of polluted information, we need to consider far more than content that mimics 'news'.[[330]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-332)

In October 2020, a hoax claim made by a spoof [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter") account, about the supposed reopening of [Woolworths](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woolworths_(United_Kingdom) "Woolworths (United Kingdom)") stores, was repeated without verification by news sites including the _[Daily Mail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily\_Mail "Daily Mail")_ and _[Daily Mirror](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daily\_Mirror "Daily Mirror")_ (and the latter's regional sister titles).[[331]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-333)

### Asia

#### Bangladesh

Fake news and digital misinformation have become major social and political concerns in Bangladesh, particularly through social media platforms, [YouTube channels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/YouTube_channels "YouTube channels"), and online news portals. According to [Rumor Scanner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rumor_Scanner_Bangladesh "Rumor Scanner Bangladesh")⁠, a leading Bangladeshi fact-checking organization, 428 pieces of misinformation were identified online in April 2026 alone, leading to the publication of 351 fact-check reports.[[332]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-334) The same organization reported that 472 misleading claims were detected in March 2026. A report published by [The Daily Star⁠](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_Star_(Bangladesh) "The Daily Star (Bangladesh)") stated that 78 fake or misleading news reports were identified after analyzing 681 reports from 164 Bangladeshi media outlets during the first six months of 2024.[[333]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-335) International fact-checking platform [AFP Fact Check](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AFP_Fact_Check "AFP Fact Check") Bangladesh⁠ has also regularly debunked false images, recycled videos, AI-generated content, and political misinformation related to Bangladesh.⁠[[334]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-336) According to Academic research misinformation spreads more rapidly in Bangladesh during elections, political crises, and periods of religious tension. A study published by Springer⁠ examined the spread of digital misinformation during the July 2024 protest movement and highlighted the growing role of fact-checking initiatives in countering false narratives.[[335]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-337)[[336]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-338)

#### China

See also: [Internet censorship in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_censorship_in_China "Internet censorship in China") and [50 Cent Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/50_Cent_Party "50 Cent Party")

Fake news during the 2016 U.S. election spread to China. Articles popularized within the United States were translated into Chinese and spread within China.[[268]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guardian12.2.16-270) The [government of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_China "Government of China") used the growing problem of fake news as a rationale for increasing [Internet censorship in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_censorship_in_China "Internet censorship in China") in November 2016.[[337]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-339) China then published an editorial in its [Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party "Communist Party") newspaper _[The Global Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Global\_Times "The Global Times")_ called "Western media's crusade against Facebook", and criticized "unpredictable" political problems posed by freedoms enjoyed by users of [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter"), [Google](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google "Google"), and [Facebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Facebook "Facebook"). China government leaders meeting in [Wuzhen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wuzhen "Wuzhen") at the third [World Internet Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Internet_Conference "World Internet Conference") in November 2016 said fake news in the U.S. election justified adding more curbs to free and open use of the Internet.
