China Deputy Minister Ren Xianliang, official at the [Cyberspace Administration of China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cyberspace_Administration_of_China "Cyberspace Administration of China"), said increasing online participation led to "harmful information" and fraud.[[338]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-340) Kam Chow Wong, a former [Hong Kong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hong_Kong "Hong Kong") law enforcement official and criminal justice professor at [Xavier University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xavier_University "Xavier University"), praised attempts in the U.S. to patrol social media.[[339]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-341)_[The Wall Street Journal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Wall\_Street\_Journal "The Wall Street Journal")_ noted China's themes of [Internet censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_censorship "Internet censorship") became more relevant at the World Internet Conference due to the outgrowth of fake news.[[340]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-342)

The issue of fake news in the 2016 United States election gave the Chinese Government a reason to further criticize Western democracy and press freedom. The Chinese government accused Western media organisations of bias, in a move apparently inspired by Trump.[[341]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-NYT_Hernandez_2017-05-07-343)

In March 2017, the _[People's Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's\_Daily "People's Daily")_, a newspaper run by the ruling [Chinese Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Communist_Party "Chinese Communist Party"), denounced news coverage of the torture of Chinese lawyer and human rights advocate Xie Yang, claiming it to be fake news.[[341]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-NYT_Hernandez_2017-05-07-343) The newspaper published a [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter") post declaring that "Foreign media reports that police tortured a detained lawyer is FAKE NEWS, fabricated to tarnish China's image". The state-owned [Xinhua News Agency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Xinhua_News_Agency "Xinhua News Agency") claimed that "the stories were essentially fake news". The Chinese government often accused Western news organizations of being biased and dishonest.[[342]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-344)

The Chinese government also claimed that there were people who posed as journalists who spread negative information on social media in order to extort payment from their victims to stop doing so. David Bandurski of University of Hong Kong's China Media Project said that this issue continued to worsen.[[343]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-345)

##### Hong Kong

See also: [Reactions to the 2019–20 Hong Kong protests §Internet activities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reactions_to_the_2019%E2%80%9320_Hong_Kong_protests#Internet_activities "Reactions to the 2019–20 Hong Kong protests")

During the [2019–20 Hong Kong protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019%E2%80%9320_Hong_Kong_protests "2019–20 Hong Kong protests"), the Chinese government has been accused for using fake news to spread misinformation regarding the protests. It includes describing protests as "riots", and "radicals" seeking independence for the city. Due to the [online censorship in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_China "Censorship in China"), citizens inside mainland China could not read news reports from some media outlets.[[344]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-346)[[345]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-347) It was also found by Facebook, Twitter and YouTube that misinformation was spread with fake accounts and advertisements by state-backed media. Large amount of accounts were suspended.[[346]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-348)

[Dot Dot News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dot_Dot_News "Dot Dot News"), a pro-Beijing online media located in Hong Kong, has been banned by Facebook for distributing fake news and hate speech.[[347]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-349)

#### India

Main article: [Fake news in India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_in_India "Fake news in India")

Fake news in India has led to violent incidents between [castes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste "Caste") and religions, interfering with public policies. It often spreads through the [smartphone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smartphone "Smartphone")[instant messenger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instant_messaging "Instant messaging")[WhatsApp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WhatsApp "WhatsApp"),[[348]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-wapo-350) which had 200 million [monthly active users](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monthly_active_users "Monthly active users") in the country as of February 2017[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Fake_news&action=edit).[[349]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-351)

#### Indonesia

Indonesia is reported to have the fourth-highest number of Facebook users in the world.[[350]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-BBC2019-352) Indonesia has seen an increase in the amount of fake news and hoaxes on social media, particularly around elections in 2014 and 2019.[[351]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Hui-353)[[352]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Paterson-354) This has been accompanied by increased polarization within the country.[[353]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Warburton-355)

During the [2014 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Indonesian_presidential_election "2014 Indonesian presidential election"), the eventual winning candidate [Joko Widodo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joko_Widodo "Joko Widodo") became a target of a smear campaign by [Prabowo Subianto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prabowo_Subianto "Prabowo Subianto")'s supporters that falsely claimed he was the child of [Indonesian Communist Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communist_Party_of_Indonesia "Communist Party of Indonesia") members, of [Chinese descent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_Indonesians "Chinese Indonesians"), and a [Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christians "Christians").[[354]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Kwok-2017-356) After Widodo won, Subianto challenged the results, making claims of widespread fraud that were not upheld. Observers found that the election was carried out fairly.[[355]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-BBC2014-357)

According to Mafindo, which tracks fake news in Indonesia, political disinformation increased by 61% between December 2018 and January 2019, leading up to the [2019 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Indonesian_general_election "2019 Indonesian general election"). Both political candidates and electoral institutions were targeted.[[350]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-BBC2019-352)[[356]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Lamb-358) Both sides formed dedicated anti-hoax groups to counterattacks on social media.[[357]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-antihoaxprabowo-359)[[358]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-360) The Indonesian government held weekly fake news briefings.[[351]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Hui-353)[[359]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-361) Once again, the losing candidate refused to accept the result and claimed that there had been fraud, without presenting any supporting evidence. Protests, rioting, and deaths of protestors were reported.[[360]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-362)

Fake news in Indonesia frequently tends to be related to alleged [Chinese imperialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_imperialism "Chinese imperialism") (including [Sinicization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinicization "Sinicization")), [Christianization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Christianization "Christianization"),[[361]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Suryadinata-363) and [communization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communization "Communization").[[362]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Varagur-364) Inflaming ethnic and political tensions is potentially deadly in Indonesia, with its recent incidences of domestic terrorism, and its long and bloody history of [anti-communist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-communism "Anti-communism"), [anti-Christian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Christians_in_the_modern_era "Persecution of Christians in the modern era") and [anti-Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sinophobia "Sinophobia") pogroms cultivated by [Suharto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suharto "Suharto")'s [U.S.-backed right-wing dictatorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Order_(Indonesia) "New Order (Indonesia)").[[354]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Kwok-2017-356)[[363]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-365)[[364]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Beech-366)

The Indonesian government, watchdog groups, and even religious organizations have taken steps to prevent the spreading of disinformation, through steps such as blocking certain websites and creating fact-check apps. The largest Islamic mass organization in Indonesia, [Nahdlatul Ulama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nahdlatul_Ulama "Nahdlatul Ulama"), has created an anti-fake news campaign called #TurnBackHoax, while other Islamic groups have defined such propagation as tantamount to sin.[[354]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Kwok-2017-356) While the government currently views criminal punishment as the last resort, officials are working hard to guarantee law enforcement will respect the freedom of expression.

#### Malaysia

In April 2018, Malaysia implemented the Anti-Fake News Bill 2018, a controversial law that deemed publishing and circulating misleading information a crime punishable by up to six years in prison and/or fines of up to 500,000 ringgit.[[365]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-malaysia-367) At implementation, the country's prime minister was [Najib Razak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Najib_Razak "Najib Razak"), whose associates were connected to the mishandling of at least $3.5 billion by a [United States Department of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice "United States Department of Justice") report.[[365]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-malaysia-367)[[366]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Paddock-2016-368) Of that sum of money, $731 million was deposited into bank accounts controlled by Razak.[[365]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-malaysia-367)[[366]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Paddock-2016-368) The convergence between the fake news law and Razak's connection to scandal was made clear by the Malaysian minister of communications and multimedia, [Salleh Said Keruak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salleh_Said_Keruak "Salleh Said Keruak"), who said that tying Razak to a specific dollar amount could be a prosecutable offense.[[365]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-malaysia-367) In the [2018 Malaysian general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Malaysian_general_election "2018 Malaysian general election"), Najib Razak lost his seat as prime minister to [Mahatir Mohammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahathir_Mohamad "Mahathir Mohamad"), who vowed to abolish the fake news law in his campaign, as the law was used to target him.[[367]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-BBC_News-2018-369)[[368]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-CNET-2018-370) After winning the election, the newly elected prime minister Mohammad has said, "Even though we support freedom of press and freedom of speech, there are limits."[[367]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-BBC_News-2018-369)[[368]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-CNET-2018-370)As of May 2018[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Fake_news&action=edit), Mohammad has supported amending the law, rather than a full abolition.[[368]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-CNET-2018-370)

Paul Bernal, a lecturer in information and technology, fears that the fake news epidemic is a "Trojan horse" for countries like Malaysia to "control uncomfortable stories".[[369]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-371) The vagueness of this law means that satirists, opinion writers, and journalists who make errors could face persecution. The law also makes it illegal to share fake news stories. In one instance, a Danish man and Malaysian citizen were arrested for posting false news stories online and were sentenced to serve a month in jail.[[370]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-372)

#### Myanmar (Burma)

In 2015, [BBC News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC_News "BBC News") reported on fake stories, using unrelated photographs and fraudulent captions, shared online in support of the [Rohingya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rohingya "Rohingya").[[371]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-373) Fake news negatively affected individuals in [Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myanmar "Myanmar"), leading to a rise in [violence against Muslims in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Persecution_of_Muslims_in_Myanmar "Persecution of Muslims in Myanmar"). Online participation surged from one percent to 20 percent of Myanmar's total populace from 2014 to 2016. Fake stories from Facebook were reprinted in paper periodicals called _Facebook_ and _The Internet_. False reporting related to practitioners of Islam in the country was directly correlated with increased attacks on [Muslims in Myanmar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Myanmar "Islam in Myanmar"). [BuzzFeed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BuzzFeed "BuzzFeed") journalist Sheera Frenkel reported fake news fictitiously stated believers in Islam acted out in violence at Buddhist locations. She documented a direct relationship between the fake news and violence against Muslim people. Frenkel noted countries that were relatively newer to Internet exposure were more vulnerable to the problems of fake news and fraud.

#### Pakistan

[Khawaja Muhammad Asif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khawaja_Muhammad_Asif "Khawaja Muhammad Asif"), the [Minister of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_Minister_of_Defence "Pakistan Minister of Defence") of [Pakistan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan "Pakistan"), threatened on [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter") to attack [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel "Israel") with nuclear weapons after a false story claiming that [Avigdor Lieberman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avigdor_Lieberman "Avigdor Lieberman"), the Israeli [Ministry of Defense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defense_(Israel) "Ministry of Defense (Israel)"), said "If Pakistan send ground troops into Syria on any pretext, we will destroy this country with a nuclear attack."[[372]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-374)[[373]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-375)

#### Philippines

Main article: [Fake news in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_in_the_Philippines "Fake news in the Philippines")

Fake news sites have become rampant for Philippine audiences, especially being shared on social media.[[374]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-cnnPhilippinesCBCPFightFakeNews-376) Politicians have started filing laws to combat fake news[[375]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-inquirerPangilinanSocMedFakeNews-377)[[376]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-cnnPhilippinesBillFiledFakeNews-378) and three Senate hearings have been held on the topic.[[377]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-senateTacklesFakeNewsPh-379)[[378]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-senateHolds2ndHearingPh-380)[[379]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-liveSenateHearing3Ph-381)

The [Catholic Church in the Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_the_Philippines "Catholic Church in the Philippines") has also released a missive speaking out against it.[[380]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-stopFakeNewsFilipinoBishops-382)

[Vera Files](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vera_Files "Vera Files") research at the end of 2017 and 2018 show that the most shared fake news in the Philippines appeared to benefit two people the most: President [Rodrigo Duterte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rodrigo_Duterte "Rodrigo Duterte") (as well as his allies) and politician [Bongbong Marcos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bongbong_Marcos "Bongbong Marcos"), with the most viral news driven by shares on networks of Facebook pages.[[381]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-veraYearEnderBenefitedMostFakeNews-383) Most Philippine-audience Facebook pages and groups spreading online disinformation also bear _Duterte_, _Marcos_ or _News_ in their names and are pro-Duterte.[[382]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-veraAdsRevealLinks-384) Online disinformation in the Philippines is overwhelmingly political as well, with most attacking groups or individuals critical of the Duterte administration.[[383]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-veraDuterteAlliesMostBenefitsDisinformation-385) Many Philippine-audience fake news websites also appear to be controlled by the same operators as they share common [Google AdSense](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_AdSense "Google AdSense") and [Google Analytics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Google_Analytics "Google Analytics") IDs.[[382]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-veraAdsRevealLinks-384)

According to media scholar Jonathan Corpus Ong, Duterte's presidential campaign is regarded as the [patient zero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Index_case "Index case") in the [current era of disinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#21st_century), having preceded widespread global coverage of the [Cambridge Analytica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cambridge_Analytica "Cambridge Analytica") scandal and [Russian trolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_Research_Agency "Internet Research Agency").[[384]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-trollsForSale-386) Fake news is so established and severe in the Philippines that Facebook's Global Politics and Government Outreach Director [Katie Harbath](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katie_Harbath "Katie Harbath") also calls it "patient zero"[[385]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-facebookKatieHarbath-387) in the global misinformation epidemic, having happened before [Brexit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brexit "Brexit"), the [Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") nomination and the [2016 US Elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_presidential_election "2016 United States presidential election").[[386]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-nytSoldiersFacebookWarFakeNews-388)

#### Singapore

See also: [Protection from Online Falsehoods and Manipulation Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protection_from_Online_Falsehoods_and_Manipulation_Act "Protection from Online Falsehoods and Manipulation Act")

[Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Singapore "Singapore") criminalizes the propagation of fake news. Under existing law, "Any person who transmits or causes to be transmitted a message which he knows to be false or fabricated shall be guilty of an offense".[[387]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-389)

On March 18, 2015, a doctored screenshot of the Prime Minister's Office website claiming the demise of [Lee Kuan Yew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Kuan_Yew "Lee Kuan Yew") went viral, and several international news agencies such as [CNN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN "CNN") and [China Central Television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China_Central_Television "China Central Television") initially reported it as news, until corrected by the Prime Minister's Office. The image was created by a student to demonstrate to his classmates how fake news could be easily created and propagated.[[388]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-390) In 2017, Singaporean news website _Mothership_ was criticized by the [Ministry of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Education_(Singapore) "Ministry of Education (Singapore)") (MOE) for propagating remarks falsely attributed to a MOE official.[[389]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-391) In addition, Minister of Law [K. Shanmugam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K._Shanmugam "K. Shanmugam") also singled out online news website _The States Times Review_ as an example of a source of fake news, as it once claimed a near-zero turnout at the state funeral of President [S. R. Nathan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/S._R._Nathan "S. R. Nathan").[[390]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-392)

Following these incidents, Shanmugam stated that the existing legalization is limited and ineffective[[391]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-393) and indicated that the government intends to introduce legislation to combat fake news in 2018.[[392]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-394) In 2017, the Ministry of Communications and Information set up _Factually_, a website intended to debunk false rumors regarding issues of public interest such as the environment, housing and transport,[[393]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-395) while in 2018, the [Parliament of Singapore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Singapore "Parliament of Singapore") formed a [Select Committee on Deliberate Online Falsehoods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Select_Committee_on_Deliberate_Online_Falsehoods "Select Committee on Deliberate Online Falsehoods") to consider new legislation to tackle fake news.[[394]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-396) On recommendations from the select committee, the Singapore government introduced the [Protection from Online Falsehoods and Manipulation Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protection_from_Online_Falsehoods_and_Manipulation_Act "Protection from Online Falsehoods and Manipulation Act") (POFMA) in April 2019.[[395]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-397)

Critics had pointed out that this bill could introduce government self-censorship and increase government control over social media.[[396]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-398) Activist platform _[The Online Citizen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Online\_Citizen "The Online Citizen")_ regarded legislation against fake news as an attempt by the government to curb the free flow of information so that only information approved by the government is disseminated to the public.[[397]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-399) In an online essay, activist and historian [Thum Ping Tjin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thum_Ping_Tjin "Thum Ping Tjin") denied that fake news was a problem in Singapore, and accused the [People's Action Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/People's_Action_Party "People's Action Party") government as the only major source of fake news, claiming that detentions made without trial during [Operation Coldstore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Coldstore "Operation Coldstore") and [Operation Spectrum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Spectrum "Operation Spectrum") were based on fake news for party political gain.[[398]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-400) Facebook and Google had opposed the introduction of the law to combat fake news, claiming that existing legislation was adequate to address the problem and that an effective way of combating misinformation is through educating citizens on how to distinguish reliable from unreliable information.[[399]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-401)

The bill was passed June 3, 2019. Commencing on October 2, 2019, the law is designed specifically to allow authorities to respond to fake news or false information through a graduated process of enforcing links to fact-checking statements, censorship of website or assets on social media platforms, and criminal charges.[[400]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-402) There have been 75 recorded instances of POFMA's usage since the law's introduction, with the latest[_[as of?](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Manual\_of\_Style/Dates\_and\_numbers#Chronological\_items "Wikipedia:Manual of Style/Dates and numbers")_] occurring on May 7, 2021.[[401]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-403)

#### South Korea

South Korean journalists and media experts lament political hostility between South and North Korea which distorts [media coverage of North Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_coverage_of_North_Korea "Media coverage of North Korea")[[402]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-404) and North Korea has attributed erroneous reporting to South Korea and United States with being critical to media organization [Chosun Ilbo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Chosun_Ilbo "The Chosun Ilbo")[[403]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-lareviewofbooks.org-405) while American journalist [Barbara Demick](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barbara_Demick "Barbara Demick") had also made similar criticisms on media coverage of North.[[403]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-lareviewofbooks.org-405)

On November 27, 2018, prosecutors raided the house of [Gyeonggi Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gyeonggi_Province "Gyeonggi Province") governor [Lee Jae-myung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lee_Jae-myung "Lee Jae-myung") amid suspicions that his wife used a pseudonymous Twitter handle to spread fake news about [President](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_South_Korea "President of South Korea")[Moon Jae-in](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moon_Jae-in "Moon Jae-in") and other political rivals of her husband.[[404]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-406)[[405]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-407)

#### Taiwan

[Taiwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taiwan "Taiwan")'s leaders, including President [Tsai Ing-wen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tsai_Ing-wen "Tsai Ing-wen") and Premier [William Lai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Lai "William Lai"), accused [China's troll army](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/50_Cent_Party "50 Cent Party") of spreading "fake news" via social media to support candidates more sympathetic to Beijing ahead of the [2018 Taiwanese local elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Taiwanese_local_elections "2018 Taiwanese local elections").[[406]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-408)[[407]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-409)[[408]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-410)

In a report in December 2015 by _[The China Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_China\_Post "The China Post")_, a fake video shared online showed people a light show purportedly made at the [Shihmen Reservoir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shihmen_Reservoir "Shihmen Reservoir"). The Northern Region Water Resources Office confirmed there was no light show at the reservoir and the event had been fabricated. The fraud led to an increase in tourist visits to the actual attraction.

According to the news updated paper from the Time World in regards the global threat to free speech, the Taiwanese government has reformed its policy on education and it will include "media literacy" as one part of school curriculum for the students. It will be included to develop the [critical-thinking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Critical_thinking "Critical thinking") skills needed while using social media. Further, the work of media literacy will also include the skills needed to analyze propaganda and sources, so students can clarify what is fake news.[[409]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-411)

#### Turkey

Fake news has become a major challenge in Turkey, particularly during elections and political crises. According to the Reuters Institute, 49% of people in Turkey reported seeing completely fabricated news stories for political or commercial purposes, compared with a 26% average across surveyed countries. The report also found that 53% had encountered news in which facts were distorted to support a particular agenda.[[410]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:3-412) Turkey's highly polarized media environment, which includes pro-government outlets such as conservative, Islamist Turkish daily newspaper [Yeni Şafak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeni_%C5%9Eafak "Yeni Şafak"),[[411]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-413) has contributed to concerns over misinformation and media bias, although misinformation is not confined to any single media organization.[[410]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:3-412)

### Americas

#### Brazil

Brazil faced increasing influence from fake news after the [2014 re-election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_Brazilian_general_election "2014 Brazilian general election") of President [Dilma Rousseff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilma_Rousseff "Dilma Rousseff") and [Rousseff's subsequent impeachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impeachment_of_Dilma_Rousseff "Impeachment of Dilma Rousseff") in August 2016. BBC Brazil reported in April 2016 that in the week surrounding one of the impeachment votes, three out of the five most-shared articles on Facebook in Brazil were fake. In 2015, reporter Tai Nalon resigned from her position at Brazilian newspaper _[Folha de S Paulo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folha\_de\_S\_Paulo "Folha de S Paulo")_ in order to start the first fact-checking website in Brazil, called _Aos Fatos_ ('To The Facts'). Nalon told _[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Guardian "The Guardian")_ there was a great deal of fake news, and hesitated to compare the problem to that experienced in the U.S.[[268]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Guardian12.2.16-270) Brazil also has a problem with fake news, and according to a survey, a greater number of people that believe fake news influenced the outcome of their elections (69%) than in the United States (47%).[[58]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Reuters-2017-58)

[![Image 18](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/ed/Jair_Bolsonaro_em_maio_de_2019.jpg/250px-Jair_Bolsonaro_em_maio_de_2019.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Jair_Bolsonaro_em_maio_de_2019.jpg)

Jair Bolsonaro

President of Brazil [Jair Bolsonaro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jair_Bolsonaro "Jair Bolsonaro") has claimed that he will not allow his government to use any of its 1.8 billion _[reais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brazilian\_real "Brazilian real")_ (US$487 million) media budget on purchases from fake news media (that is, media that does not support him).[[412]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-414) The _[BBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BBC "BBC")_ reported that Bolsonaro's campaign declared media associating his campaign to the "extreme right" were themselves fake news.[[413]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-415) In 2020, [Brazil's Supreme Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Federal_Court "Supreme Federal Court") began an investigation into a purported disinformation campaign by Bolsonaro supporters.[[414]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-416) The Brazilian President claimed the investigation was "unconstitutional", and any restriction of fake news was an act of censorship.[[415]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-417) After an order by the Brazilian Supreme Court, Facebook had removed "dozens" of fake accounts that were directly linked to Bolsonaro's offices and his sons, and which were directed against politicians and media that opposed the President.[[416]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-418)[[417]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-419) A video of Bolsonaro falsely claiming that the anti-malarial drug [hydroxychloroquine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydroxychloroquine "Hydroxychloroquine") has been working everywhere against the coronavirus was also taken down by Facebook and Twitter.[[418]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-420) In regards to the COVID-19 pandemic, he has accused his political opponents of exaggerating the severity of the virus. He gave a speech in 2021 in which he claimed that the virus was not as bad as the media made it out to be, and it was "fantasy" created by the media.[[419]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-421)

In the wake of the uptick in Amazon fires of 2019, it became clear that many of the forest fire photos that went viral were fake news.[[420]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-422)[[421]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-afp.com-423) Emmanuel Macron, president of France, tweeted a picture taken by a photographer who died in 2003, for example.[[421]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-afp.com-423)[[422]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-NYTMacronDeadPhotographer-424)[[423]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-425)

#### Canada

Fake news online was brought to the attention of Canadian politicians in November 2016, as they debated helping assist local newspapers. Member of [Parliament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parliament_of_Canada "Parliament of Canada") for [Vancouver Centre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vancouver_Centre "Vancouver Centre")[Hedy Fry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hedy_Fry "Hedy Fry") specifically discussed fake news as an example of ways in which publishers on the Internet are less accountable than print media. Discussion in parliament contrasted the increase of fake news online with the downsizing of Canadian newspapers and the impact on democracy in Canada. Representatives from Facebook Canada attended the meeting and told members of Parliament they felt it was their duty to assist individuals in gathering data online.[[424]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-426)

In January 2017, the [Conservative leadership campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Conservative_Party_of_Canada_leadership_election "2017 Conservative Party of Canada leadership election") of [Kellie Leitch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kellie_Leitch "Kellie Leitch") admitted to spreading fake news, including false claims that [Justin Trudeau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justin_Trudeau "Justin Trudeau") was financing [Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamas "Hamas"). The campaign manager claimed he spread the news in order to provoke negative reactions so that he could determine those who "aren't real Conservatives".[[425]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-427)

#### Colombia

In the fall of 2016, WhatsApp spread fake news that impacted votes critical to Colombian history.[[426]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Uribe-2018-428) One of the lies spreading rapidly through WhatsApp was that Colombian citizens would receive less pension so former guerrilla fighters would get money.[[426]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Uribe-2018-428) The misinformation initially began in a question as to whether WhatsApp users approved of the peace accord deal between the national government and the Revolutionary Armed Forces of Colombia (FARC) or not. The peace accord would end five decades of war between paramilitary groups (rebel forces) and the Colombian government that resulted in millions of deaths and displaced citizens throughout the country. A powerful influence of votes was the "no" campaign; the "no" campaign was to convince citizens of Colombia to not accept the peace accord because it would be letting the rebel group off "too easily".[[427]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Garavito-2016-429) Uribe, former president of Colombia and of the democratic party, led the "no" campaign. Santos, president in 2016, took liberal approaches during his presidency. Santos won a Nobel Peace Prize in 2016 for his efforts towards a peace accord with rebel forces.[[218]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Santos_Calderon-2019-220) In addition, Uribe naturally had opposing views to those of Santos.[[218]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Santos_Calderon-2019-220)[[426]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Uribe-2018-428) Furthermore, other news spread through WhatsApp were easily misinterpreted by the public, including that Santo's scheme was to put Colombia under harsh rule like Cuba and chaos like Venezuela (under Hugo Chávez), though the logistics were never explained.[[426]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Uribe-2018-428) In an interview with Juan Carlos Vélez, the "no" campaign manager, he says their strategy was that "We discovered the viral power of social networks."[[426]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Uribe-2018-428) In addition, the "yes" campaign also took part in spreading fake news through WhatsApp. For instance, a photoshopped image of democratic senator Everth Bustamante him holding a sign reading "I don't want guerrillas in congress" spread to show hypocrisy. This would be seen as hypocritical because he was a former left-wing M-19 guerrilla.[[426]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Uribe-2018-428) The "no" campaign strongly influenced votes throughout Colombia. "Yes" votes were strong in areas with the highest number of victims and "no" votes in areas influenced by Uribe. As a result, "no" votes totaled 50.2%, compared to 49.8% "yes" votes.[[427]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Garavito-2016-429) The result of fake news spreading on WhatsApp included changes to the app by journalist [Juanita León](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Juanita_Le%C3%B3n "Juanita León"), who invented the WhatsApp "lie detector" in January 2017 to fight fake news within the app.[[426]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Uribe-2018-428) Although the accord was eventually signed, the WhatsApp incident further prolonged the accord and spread controversial views among citizens.

#### Mexico

Main article: [Peñabot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pe%C3%B1abot "Peñabot")

In Mexico, people tend to rely heavily on social media and direct social contact as news sources, over television and print.[[428]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Reuters2023-430) Usage of and trust in all types of news sources (including social media) declined from 2017 to 2023. As of 2023, the most-used media companies in Mexico were [TV Azteca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TV_Azteca "TV Azteca") news and [Televisa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Televisa "Televisa").[[428]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Reuters2023-430) Televisa is both Mexico's largest television network and the largest media network in the Spanish-speaking world.[[429]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Moore-431) The three most frequently used social media platforms were Facebook, YouTube, and WhatsApp.[[428]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Reuters2023-430)

Prior to 2012, the country's major television networks were central to [political communication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Mexico "Politics of Mexico") in Mexico. They were also closely connected to the long-dominant [PRI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Institutional_Revolutionary_Party "Institutional Revolutionary Party") political party.[[430]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-G%C3%A1miz-432) Televiso has been criticized by journalists and academics for misrepresentation and manipulation of information, and attacks on opponents.[[431]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Lara-433) Televiso shaped the campaign of [Enrique Peña Nieto](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Enrique_Pe%C3%B1a_Nieto "Enrique Peña Nieto")[[432]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Meikle-434) whose Presidency from 2012 to 2018 was marked by scandals and decreased trust in television and print media.[[430]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-G%C3%A1miz-432)

By the [2012 Mexican general election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Mexican_general_election "2012 Mexican general election"), coordinated online disinformation campaigns were part of an "explosion of digital politics" in Mexico.[[432]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Meikle-434)[[433]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Watts-435)[[434]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Armstrong-436) Mexican politicians used digital strategies and algorithms to boost their apparent popularity and undermine or overwhelm opposing messages.[[432]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Meikle-434) Attempts to "hack" the "[attention economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attention_economy "Attention economy")" were made to amplify false narratives, capture attention and dominate discourse.[[435]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Goldhaber-437)[[436]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-boyd-438) A network of trolls was formed as early as 2009, to be activated as needed in support of Peña Nieto. Estimates of the number of people involved, and how many were paid, vary widely from 20,000 to 100,000 people. Bots were also used to amplify messages and create "false universes of followers".[[432]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Meikle-434) Opposing voices were drowned out by generating large volumes of meaningless responses from "ghost followers".[[432]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Meikle-434) In one incident that was analyzed, 50 spam accounts generated 1,000 tweets per day.[[437]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Verkamp-439)

Government activities following Peña Nieto's election included the amplification of support for controversial government initiatives.[[432]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Meikle-434) After the [killing or disappearance of a group of activist students](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iguala_mass_kidnapping "Iguala mass kidnapping") in 2014, algorithms were used to sabotage trending hashtags on Twitter such as #_YaMeCanse_'IHaveHadEnough'.[[432]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Meikle-434) Bots and trolls have also been used for threats and personal attacks.[[432]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Meikle-434) Between 2017 and 2019, the hashtag #_SalarioRosa_'Pink Salary for Vulnerability' was associated with political figure [Alfredo del Mazo Maza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alfredo_del_Mazo_Maza "Alfredo del Mazo Maza") and pushed to the top of Twitter's trending list through [astroturfing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astroturfing "Astroturfing"), creating an appearance of grassroots support. Photographs of women were associated with fake accounts to create the impression that women were engaged in the discussion, "mimicking conversation".[[438]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Espinoza-440)

As of 2018, 76–80% of people surveyed in Mexico worried about false information or fake news being used as a weapon, the highest rate of any country in the world.[[439]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-441) During the [2018 election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Mexican_general_election "2018 Mexican general election"), bot battles between candidates drowned out conversations by posting attacks, rumors, unsubstantiated claims, and [deepfaked](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deepfake "Deepfake") videos.[[434]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Armstrong-436)[[440]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Bandeira-442) Forty percent of the election-related tweets on Twitter mentioned [Andrés Manuel López Obrador](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9s_Manuel_L%C3%B3pez_Obrador "Andrés Manuel López Obrador") (AMLO). None of his opponents reached twenty percent of the tweets.[[440]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Bandeira-442):21 Automation and artificial amplification used both commercial and political bot services. At one point at least ten pro- and anti-AMLO bots posted over a thousand tweets in a matter of hours using the hashtag #AMLOFest.[[440]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Bandeira-442):24

The collaborative journalism project _Verificado 2018_ was established to address misinformation during the 2018 presidential election. It involved at least eighty organizations, including local and national media outlets, universities and civil society and advocacy groups. The group researched online claims and political statements and published joint verifications. During the course of the election, they produced over 400 notes and 50 videos documenting false claims and suspect sites, and tracked instances where fake news went viral.[[434]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Armstrong-436)[[440]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Bandeira-442):23 Verificado.mx received 5.4 million visits during the election, with its partner organizations registering millions more.[[440]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Bandeira-442):25

One of the tactics they observed was the promotion of fabricated polls that exaggerated the support for various candidates. The fake polls were claimed to have been carried out by sources such as _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_New\_York\_Times "The New York Times")_ and _[El Universal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/El\_Universal\_(Mexico\_City) "El Universal (Mexico City)")_, one of Mexico's top newspapers.[[434]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Armstrong-436) Another tactic was to share encrypted messages via WhatsApp, In response, Verificado set up a hotline where WhatsApp users could submit messages for verification and debunking. Over 10,000 users subscribed to Verificado's hotline.[[434]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Armstrong-436) Fake messages included false information about where and how to vote.[[434]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Armstrong-436)[[440]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Bandeira-442):25 One campaign urged anti-AMLO voters to check boxes for both of his opponents—an action that would result in disqualification of the vote.[[434]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Armstrong-436)

Since being elected president in 2018, Andrés Manuel López Obrador (AMLO)'s behavior toward the media has been adversarial. He has used his morning addresses or _mañaneras_ to target journalists such as [Carmen Aristegui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carmen_Aristegui "Carmen Aristegui"), [Carlos Loret de Mola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carlos_Loret_de_Mola "Carlos Loret de Mola"), and [Victor Trujillo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Trujillo "Victor Trujillo"). [Article 19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Article_19 "Article 19"), an international human rights organization, considers the Mexican government to be using a "strategy of disinformation" while limiting access to public sources of information. Article 19 estimates that 26.5% of the public information provided by the Mexican government is false.[[428]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Reuters2023-430) As of 2019, Mexico became the most dangerous country in the world for journalists,[[440]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Bandeira-442)[[441]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Carrie-443) with a higher death toll than active war zones.[[442]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Kahn-444)

During the pandemic, use of social media platforms such as WhatsApp and Facebook increased. In Mexico, the user base of the video app TikTok tripled from 2019 to 2021, reaching 17 million viewers.[[443]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Pi%C3%B1a-Garc%C3%ADa-445) Patterns of Twitter activity during the pandemic suggest that astroturfing was used to create an appearance of widespread grassroots support for AMLO and for [Hugo López-Gatell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hugo_L%C3%B3pez-Gatell "Hugo López-Gatell") at a time when both men were being criticized for their handling of the [COVID-19 pandemic in Mexico](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Mexico "COVID-19 pandemic in Mexico").[[443]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Pi%C3%B1a-Garc%C3%ADa-445)

#### United States

Main articles: [Fake news in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_in_the_United_States "Fake news in the United States"), [Fake news websites in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fake_news_websites_in_the_United_States "Fake news websites in the United States"), and [Media bias in the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_bias_in_the_United_States "Media bias in the United States")

### Middle East and Africa

#### Armenia

According to a report by [openDemocracy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/OpenDemocracy "OpenDemocracy") in 2020, the Armenian website Medmedia.am was spreading disinformation about the coronavirus pandemic, calling [COVID-19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coronavirus_disease_2019 "Coronavirus disease 2019") a "fake pandemic" and warning Armenians to refuse future vaccine programmes. The website is led by Gevorg Grigoryan, a doctor who has been critical of the Armenian government's health ministry and its vaccine programmes, and has a history of anti-LGBT statements, including remarks posted on Facebook in which he called for gay people to be burned. _The Guardian_ said the site was launched with the unwitting help of a US State Department grant intended to promote democracy.[[444]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-446)

#### Israel and Palestinian territories

See also: [Pallywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pallywood "Pallywood")

In 1996, people were killed in the [Western Wall Tunnel riots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Wall_Tunnel_riots "Western Wall Tunnel riots") in reaction to fake news accounts.[[445]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-447) In April 2018, Palestinian-Israeli football team [Bnei Sakhnin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bnei_Sakhnin_F.C. "Bnei Sakhnin F.C.") threatened to sue Israeli Prime Minister [Benjamin Netanyahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Benjamin_Netanyahu "Benjamin Netanyahu") for libel after he claimed fans booed during a minute of silence for Israeli flash-flood victims.

In a social media post, Netanyahu blasted various Israeli news critical of him, as fake news including [Channel 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_2_(Israel) "Channel 2 (Israel)"), [Channel 10](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Ten_(Israel) "Channel Ten (Israel)"), _[Haaretz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haaretz "Haaretz")_ and [Ynet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ynetnews "Ynetnews") the same day U.S. President Trump decried "fake news".

The Palestinian Islamist political organization [Hamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamas "Hamas") published a political program in 2017 intended to ease its position on Israel. Among other things, this charter accepted the borders of the Palestinian state circa the [Six-Day War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Six-Day_War "Six-Day War") of 1967.[[446]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Dewan-2017-448) Although this document is an advancement from their previous 1988 charter, which called for the destruction of the State of Israel, it still does not recognize Israel as a legitimate independent nation.[[446]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Dewan-2017-448) In a May 2017 video, Prime Minister of Israel, Benjamin Netanyahu responded to the coverage of this event by news outlets such as _[Al Jazeera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al\_Jazeera\_Media\_Network "Al Jazeera Media Network")_, [CNN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN "CNN"), _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_New\_York\_Times "The New York Times")_ and _[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Guardian "The Guardian")_, labeling their reporting "fake news".[[447]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-449) He specifically disagreed with the notion that Hamas had accepted the state of Israel within their new charter, and called this "a complete distortion of the truth". Instead he said, "The new Hamas document says Israel has no right to exist." _Haaretz_ fact-checked the video, stating, "Netanyahu, following in the footsteps of Trump, is deliberately twisting the definition of 'fake news' to serve his own needs."[[448]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-450) In a later speech, addressed to his supporters, Netanyahu responded to allegations against him: "The fake news industry is at its peak... Look, for example, how they cover with unlimited enthusiasm, every week, the left-wing demonstration. The same demonstrations whose goal is to apply improper pressure on law enforcement authorities so they will file an indictment at any price." _[The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Washington\_Post "The Washington Post")_ likened his use of the term _fake news_ for describing left-wing media to Donald Trump's similar statements during the [2016 United States election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_United_States_elections "2016 United States elections") cycle.[[449]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-451)

In studies conducted by Yifat Media Check Ltd. and Hamashrokit ('The Whistle', a fact-checking organization), they found that over 70% of statements made by Israeli political leaders were not accurate.[[450]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-452)

Some of the fake news Israel has been the victim of includes [Israel-related animal conspiracy theories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel-related_animal_conspiracy_theories "Israel-related animal conspiracy theories"), which claim Israel is using various animals to spy on or attack others with.[[451]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-453)

#### Saudi Arabia

According to the _Global News_, [Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabia "Saudi Arabia")'s state-owned television spread fake news about [Canada](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canada "Canada"). In August 2018, Canada's _Global News_ reported that state-owned television _[Al Arabiya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al\_Arabiya "Al Arabiya")_, "has suggested that Canada is the worst country in the world for women, that it has the highest suicide rate and that it treats its Indigenous people the way Myanmar treats the Rohingya—a Muslim minority massacred and driven out of Myanmar en masse last year."[[452]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-454)

In October 2018, [Twitter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Twitter "Twitter") has suspended a number of [bot accounts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_bot "Internet bot") that appeared to be spreading pro-Saudi rhetoric about the disappearance of Saudi opposition journalist [Jamal Khashoggi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jamal_Khashoggi "Jamal Khashoggi").[[453]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-455)[[454]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-456)

According to _Newsweek_, Saudi Arabia's Office of Public Prosecution tweeted that "producing rumors or fake news [that Saudi Arabia's government was involved in the disappearance of Khashoggi] that would affect the public order or public security or sending or resending it via social media or any technical means" is punishable "by five years and a fine of 3 million [riyals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_riyal "Saudi riyal")".[[455]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-457)

[Iranian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran "Iran")-backed Twitter accounts have spread sensational fake news and rumours about Saudi Arabia.[[456]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-458)

On August 1, 2019, Facebook identified hundreds of accounts that were running a covert network on behalf of government of the [Kingdom of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabia "Saudi Arabia") to spread fake news and attack regional rivals. The social media giant removed more than 350 accounts, pages and groups with nearly 1.4 million followers.[[457]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-459) Along with Facebook, these accounts were involved in "coordinated inauthentic behavior" on [Instagram](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instagram "Instagram") as well. According to a Facebook blog post, the network was running two different political agendas, one on behalf of Saudi Arabia and the other for the [United Arab Emirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Emirates "United Arab Emirates") and [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egypt "Egypt").[[458]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-460)

#### Syria

In February 2017, [Amnesty International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amnesty_International "Amnesty International") reported that up to 13,000 people had been hanged in a Syrian prison as part of an "extermination" campaign. Syrian president [Bashar al-Assad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bashar_al-Assad "Bashar al-Assad") questioned the credibility of Amnesty International and called the report "fake news" fabricated to undermine the government. "You can forge anything these days—we are living in a fake news era."[[459]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-461)

Russia ran a disinformation campaign during the [Syrian Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Civil_War "Syrian Civil War") to discredit the humanitarian rescue organisation [White Helmets](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Helmets_(Syrian_Civil_War) "White Helmets (Syrian Civil War)"), and to discredit reports and images of children and other civilian bombing victims. This was done to weaken criticism of Russia's involvement in the war.[[460]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-462) The United Nations and international chemical inspectors found Bashar al-Assad responsible for use of chemical weapons,[[461]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-463) which was called "fake news" by Russia. Russia promoted various contradictory claims that no chemicals were present, or attributing the chemical attacks to other countries or groups.[[462]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-464)[[463]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-465)[[464]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-466)[[465]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-467)[[466]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-468)

#### United Arab Emirates

The [United Arab Emirates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Arab_Emirates "United Arab Emirates") (UAE) had been funding [non-profit organizations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-profit_organizations "Non-profit organizations"), [think tanks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Think_tanks "Think tanks") and [contributors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freelancer "Freelancer") of [journalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Journalism "Journalism"), including [Foundation for Defense of Democracies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foundation_for_Defense_of_Democracies "Foundation for Defense of Democracies") (FDD) and the [Middle East Forum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_East_Forum "Middle East Forum") (MEF), which further paid journalists spreading fake information to defame countries like [Qatar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatar "Qatar"). In 2020, a researcher at FDD, Benjamin Weinthal, and fellow at MEF, [Jonathan Spyer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Spyer "Jonathan Spyer"), contributed an article on _[Fox News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fox\_News "Fox News")_ to promote a negative image of Qatar, in an attempt to stain its [diplomatic relations with the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatar%E2%80%93United_States_relations "Qatar–United States relations").[[467]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-469)

#### Egypt

According to _[The Daily Telegraph](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Daily\_Telegraph "The Daily Telegraph")_, an Egyptian official suggested in 2010 that the Israeli spy agency [Mossad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mossad "Mossad") could have been behind a fatal shark attack in [Sharm el-Sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharm_el-Sheikh "Sharm el-Sheikh").[[468]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-470) It was estimated by the Egyptian Parliament's Communication and Information Technology Committee that in 2017, 53,000 false rumors had been spread primarily through social media in 60 days.[[469]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-471)

#### South Africa

A wide range of South African media sources have reported fake news as a growing problem and tool to both increase distrust in the media, discredit political opponents, and divert attention from corruption.[[470]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-472) Media outlets owned by the [Gupta family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gupta_family "Gupta family") have been noted by other South African media organisations such as _[The Huffington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Huffington\_Post "The Huffington Post")_ (South Africa), _[Sunday Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Sunday\_Times\_(South\_Africa) "The Sunday Times (South Africa)"),_[Radio 702](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_702 "Radio 702"), and _[City Press](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City\_Press\_(South\_Africa) "City Press (South Africa)")_ for targeting them.[[471]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-473) Individuals targeted include Finance Minister [Pravin Gordhan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pravin_Gordhan "Pravin Gordhan") who was seen as blocking Gupta attempts at [state capture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_capture "State capture") with accusations levelled against Gordhan of promoting state capture for "[white monopoly capital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_monopoly_capital "White monopoly capital")".[[472]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-474)[[473]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-475)

The [African National Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/African_National_Congress "African National Congress") (ANC) was taken to court by Sihle Bolani for unpaid work she did during the election on the ANC's behalf. In court papers Bolani stated that the ANC used her to launch and run a covert R50 million fake news and [disinformation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Disinformation "Disinformation") campaign during the [2016 municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_South_African_municipal_elections "2016 South African municipal elections") with the intention of discrediting opposition parties.[[474]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-476)[[475]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-477)[[476]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-478)

### Oceania

#### Australia

The Australian Parliament initiated investigation into "fake news" regarding issues surrounding fake news that occurred during the 2016 United States election. The inquiry looked at several major areas in Australia to find audiences most vulnerable to fake news, by considering the impact on traditional journalism, and by evaluating the liability of online advertisers and by regulating the spreading the hoaxes. This act of parliament is meant to combat the threat of social media spreading fake news.[[477]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-479)

The Australian Code of Practice on Disinformation and Misinformation commenced on 22 February 2021, around 12 months after the Australian Government asked digital platforms to develop a voluntary code to address disinformation and misinformation and assist users of their services to more easily identify the reliability, trustworthiness and source of news content. The request is part of a broader Australian Government strategy to reform the technology and information dissemination landscape. The Australian Communications and Media Authority (ACMA) oversaw the development of the code. In its 2021 report to government on the code, ACMA recommended that the government introduce legislation to grant ACMA powers to enforce the code, to request transparency reports and other information from digital platforms about their misinformation measures and to conduct investigations.[[478]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-480) Responding to the report, the [Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coalition_(Australia) "Coalition (Australia)") government indicated in March 2022 that it would proceed with legislation. Following the [2022 federal election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Australian_federal_election "2022 Australian federal election"), the new [Labor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australian_Labor_Party "Australian Labor Party") government continued the legislative program and in June 2023 a draft bill was released for public consultation.[[479]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:1-481) Despite their earlier support for regulation, the Liberal–National coalition, now in opposition, voiced concerns that granting ACMA powers to regulate misinformation would endanger freedom of speech. There was also significant opposition to the bill from right-leaning media outlets, while experts voiced concerns about the design of the bill. The consultation on the legislation received 2418 public submissions and over 20,000 comments, many opposing the bill.[[479]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-:1-481) In September 2024, a revised bill was introduced to parliament and immediately referred to the Senate Communications Legislation Committee, which conducted an inquiry into the bill. Despite including significant changes to better protect freedom of expression, the bill failed to gain sufficient support to pass through the Senate and was withdrawn by the government on 23 November.[[480]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-482)

A well-known case of fabricated news in [Australia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Australia "Australia") happened in 2009 when a report, _Deception detection across Australian populations_, by a "Levitt Institute" was widely cited on news websites across the country, claiming that [Sydney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sydney "Sydney") was the most naive city, despite the fact that the report itself contained a clue: amidst the mathematical gibberish, there was a statement: "These results were completely made up to be fictitious material through a process of modified truth and credibility nodes."[[481]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-483)

## Misuse of term

### United States

See also: [False or misleading statements by Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/False_or_misleading_statements_by_Donald_Trump "False or misleading statements by Donald Trump")

[![Image 19](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Donald_Trump_official_portrait.jpg/250px-Donald_Trump_official_portrait.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Donald_Trump_official_portrait.jpg)

Donald Trump frequently mentioned fake news on Twitter to criticize the media in the United States, including [CNN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CNN "CNN") and _[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_New\_York\_Times "The New York Times")_.

In his political career and during his presidency, [Donald Trump](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") often resorted to the term "fake news" as an attack on news coverage that he deemed to be inaccurate or disparaging. In 2018, the Washington Post reported that Trump has used the word to describe anything he didn't like, not just fake news.[[482]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-484)A well-documented instance of this happened the 9th May, 2018 when Trump tweeted that "91%" of network news about him was negative, and that the negative news is "Fake". He also raised the issue of withdrawing the credentials of the media organizations.[[483]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-485)

The Washington Post's Philip Bump read into the tweet the sense that negative coverage equaled fake news, as Trump put it.In addition, Trump frequently labeled certain news outlets as "fake news" and, on a few occasions, as the "enemy of the people".His use of the term is thus being debated as part of the wider semantic change from "fake news" referring to fabricated stories, to "fake news" being used to denote 'disputed' or 'unfavorable' reporting.[[484]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-486).[[485]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-487)

The Fact Checker from The Washington Post recorded thousands of false or misleading statements made by Trump during his time in office. By June 2018 it had entered 3251 of them into its database in the first 497 days of his tenure; the database was updated afterwards.[[486]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-488)

**Donald J. Trump**

(@realDonaldTrump)

 tweeted:

> The Fake News is working overtime. Just reported that, despite the tremendous success we are having with the economy & all things else, 91% of the Network News about me is negative (Fake). Why do we work so hard in working with the media when it is corrupt? Take away credentials?

May 9, 2018[[487]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-TweetrealDonaldTrump-489)

[Chris Cillizza](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chris_Cillizza "Chris Cillizza") described the tweet on CNN as an "accidental" revelation about Trump's "'fake news' attacks", and wrote: "The point can be summed up in these two words from Trump: 'negative (Fake).' To Trump, those words mean the same thing. Negative news coverage is fake news. Fake news is negative news coverage."[[488]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Cillizza_5/9/2018-490) Other writers made similar comments about the tweet. Dara Lind wrote in _[Vox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vox\_(website) "Vox (website)")_: "It's nice of Trump to admit, explicitly, what many skeptics have suspected all along: When he complains about 'fake news,' he doesn't actually mean 'news that is untrue'; he means news that is personally inconvenient to Donald Trump."[[489]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Lind_5/9/2018-491)[Jonathan Chait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jonathan_Chait "Jonathan Chait") wrote in _[New York](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New\_York\_(magazine) "New York (magazine)")_ magazine: "Trump admits he calls all negative news 'fake'.": "In a tweet this morning, Trump casually opened a window into the source code for his method of identifying liberal media bias. Anything that's negative is, by definition, fake."[[490]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Chait_5/9/2018-492) Philip Bump wrote in _[The Washington Post](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Washington\_Post "The Washington Post")_: "The important thing in that tweet... is that he makes explicit his view of what constitutes fake news. It's negative news. Negative. (Fake.)"[[491]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Bump_5/9/2018-493) In an interview with [Lesley Stahl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lesley_Stahl "Lesley Stahl"), before the cameras were turned on, Trump explained why he attacks the press: "You know why I do it? I do it to discredit you all and demean you all so that when you write negative stories about me no one will believe you."[[492]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Mangan_5/22/2018-494)

Author and literary critic [Michiko Kakutani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michiko_Kakutani "Michiko Kakutani") has described developments in the right-wing media and websites:

> "Fox News and the planetary system of right-wing news sites that would orbit it and, later, [Breitbart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breitbart "Breitbart"), were particularly adept at weaponizing such arguments and exploiting the increasingly partisan fervor animating the Republican base: They accused the media establishment of 'liberal bias', and substituted their own right-wing views as 'fair and balanced'—a redefinition of terms that was a harbinger of Trump's hijacking of 'fake news' to refer not to alt-right conspiracy theories and Russian troll posts, but to real news that he perceived as inconvenient or a threat to himself."[[493]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Woods_6/20/2018-495)

In September 2018, [National Public Radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Public_Radio "National Public Radio") noted that Trump has expanded his use of the terms _fake_ and _phony_ to "an increasingly wide variety of things he doesn't like": "The range of things Trump is declaring fake is growing too. Last month he tweeted about "fake books", "the fake dossier", "fake CNN", and he added a new claim—that Google search results are "RIGGED" to mostly show only negative stories about him." They graphed his expanding use in columns labeled: "Fake news", "Fake (other)" and "Phony".[[494]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-Keith_9/4/2018-496)

### Russia

Main article: [Russian fake news laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_fake_news_laws "Russian fake news laws")

See also: [Russian 2019 Fake News Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_2019_Fake_News_Law "Russian 2019 Fake News Law")

See also: [Russian 2020 COVID-19 Fake News Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_2020_COVID-19_Fake_News_Law "Russian 2020 COVID-19 Fake News Law")

See also: [Russian 2022 war censorship laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_2022_war_censorship_laws "Russian 2022 war censorship laws")

In March 2019, Russia passed a [bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_2019_Fake_News_Law "Russian 2019 Fake News Law") to ban websites from spreading what the Russian government considers false information.[[495]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-497) In addition to tackling fake news, the [new legislation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_2019_Disrespect_to_Authorities_Law "Russian 2019 Disrespect to Authorities Law") specifically punishes any sources or websites for publishing materials that insult the state, the symbol of the government or other political figures. For repeated offenders, they would receive a 15-day jail sentence.[[496]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-498)

During the [Russian invasion of Ukraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_invasion_of_Ukraine "Russian invasion of Ukraine"), the Russian government [passed a law prohibiting "fake news"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_2022_war_censorship_laws "Russian 2022 war censorship laws") regarding the Russian military, which was broadly defined as any information that is deemed by the Russian government to be false, including the use of the terms _invasion_ and _war_ to refer to the invasion.[[497]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-499)[[498]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-500) Violations of the law are punishable with up to 15 years of imprisonment. International news organizations in multiple countries ceased operating in Russia and journalists emigrated from Russia en masse after the law was passed, while some domestic non-state news organizations were blocked by the Russian government.[[499]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-501)[[500]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_disorder#cite_note-502)

## Further reading

*   Bounegru, Liliana; Gray, Jonathan; Venturini, Tommaso; Mauri, Michele (January 8, 2018). [_A field guide to "fake news" and other information disorders_](http://fakenews.publicdatalab.org/). Amsterdam: Public Data Lab._An open access guide exploring the use of digital methods to study false viral news, political memes, trolling practices and their social life online._
*   Brotherton, Rob (2020). _Bad News: Why We Fall for Fake News_. Bloomsbury Sigma. [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)")[978-1-4729-6288-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-4729-6288-1 "Special:BookSources/978-1-4729-6288-1").
*   Cortada, James W. and William Aspray. _Fake news nation: The long history of lies and misinterpretations in America_ (Rowman & Littlefield Publishers, 2019) [online review](http://www.h-net.org/reviews/showpdf.php?id=55213); also [excerpt of book](https://www.amazon.com/Fake-News-Nation-History-Misinterpretations/dp/1538131102/)
*   Higgins, Andrew et al. ["Inside a fake news sausage factory: 'This is all about income'"](https://www.nytimes.com/2016/11/25/world/europe/fake-news-donald-trump-hillary-clinton-georgia.html)_[The New York Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_New\_York\_Times "The New York Times")_ November 25, 2016
*   Horner, Paul (2017). [An online journal by satirist Paul Horner, listing all of his best hoaxes and fake news over the past 20 years. However, his satire was often spread far and wide by uncritical people believing it was true.](https://web.archive.org/web/20170426081655/http://newsexaminer.net/paul-horner-news/paul-horner-news-internet-news-satirist-writer-news-examiner/)
*   Malik, Shahnawaz Ahmed, [Fake news: Legal analysis of false and misleading news and cyber propaganda](https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=3660324) (February 5, 2019). AD VALOREM- Journal of Law: Volume 6: Issue II: Part-III: April–June 2019: ISSN: 2348–5485.
*   Arun, Chinmayi, [On WhatsApp, rumours, lynchings, and the Indian Government](https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=3336127) (January 3, 2019). Economic & Political Weekly vol. lIV no. 6.
*   Nagar, Itisha and Gill, Simran, [Head is where the herd is: Fake news and effect of online social conformity on Islamophobia in Indians](https://ssrn.com/abstract=3651297). SSHO-D-20-00611, [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_(identifier) "Doi (identifier)"):[10.2139/ssrn.3651297](https://doi.org/10.2139%2Fssrn.3651297)
*   Posetti, Julie; Matthews, Alice. ["A short guide to the history of 'fake news' and disinformation"](https://www.icfj.org/sites/default/files/2018-07/A%20Short%20Guide%20to%20History%20of%20Fake%20News%20and%20Disinformation_ICFJ%20Final.pdf)(PDF). _International Center for Journalists_.
*   Robson, David (April 1, 2019). ["Why smart people are more likely to believe fake news"](https://www.theguardian.com/books/2019/apr/01/why-smart-people-are-more-likely-to-believe-fake-news). _[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The\_Guardian "The Guardian")_.
*   Schumacher, Elizabeth (January 4, 2018). ["Fake news 'casts wide net but has little effect'"](http://www.dw.com/en/fake-news-casts-wide-net-but-has-little-effect/a-42029486). [Deutsche Welle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deutsche_Welle "Deutsche Welle").
